## **Mother Juliana,**
<note>Known today as Julian of Norwich (c. 1343 – c. 1416), she was an English mystic and the author of the first book in the English language known to have been written by a woman.</note>

<note>An "anchorete" (or anchoress) was a religious recluse who lived in a permanent cell attached to a church—in Julian's case, St Julian's Church in Norwich—to devote themselves entirely to prayer and spiritual counsel.</note>

<meta>This page begins the Preface to the 1864 edition of Julian of Norwich's work, following the printer's information. It introduces the reader to Julian's character and the spiritual and historical significance of her writings from a 19th-century perspective.</meta>

# PREFACE

## TO THE PRESENT EDITION.

---

**THESE** Revelations of Mother Juliana <note>Mother Juliana refers to Julian of Norwich (c. 1343 – after 1416), a celebrated English mystic and anchorite. She is the first woman known to have written a book in the English language.</note> present to us a beautiful picture of a devout soul, who lived nearer to God than most persons in our day believe possible. Translations of books of a similar character from the German, Spanish, and Italian, have recently enriched our literature, but we know of no spiritual writer who has combined such rare thought, warmth of piety, and charming simplicity, as our English nun. <note>While the editor calls her a "nun," Julian was technically an <term>anchorite</term> (a person who lives in total shared seclusion in a cell attached to a church to focus on prayer).</note> There is no attempt at composition, <note>In this context, "composition" refers to formal literary artifice or intentional styling; the editor suggests her writing is raw and authentic.</note> no mere reproduction of remembered thought, but the very heart of a <term>contemplative soul</term> (a person dedicated to deep, silent prayer and the inner experience of God), whose inspirations, whether natural or above nature, are fresh and divine.

The book will have a special interest, moreover, for a distinct class. It possesses an historical value,

<summary>This preface introduces Julian of Norwich's "Revelations," praising her spiritual depth and the authentic, simple nature of her writing compared to other European mystics. The editor highlights the book's value as both a devotional guide and a significant historical document.</summary>
<keywords>Julian of Norwich, Mother Juliana, mysticism, 19th-century preface, contemplative life, English literature, spirituality, anchorite</keywords>

<page-num>vi</page-num>

# PREFACE TO THE PRESENT EDITION.

showing what hearts beat in English cloisters <note>A "cloister" is a covered walkway in a monastery or convent, often used as a metaphor for the religious life or the buildings where monks and nuns lived.</note> in the olden time, and how sweetly the voice of piety sounded in our good old Saxon tongue <note>The editor uses "Saxon tongue" to refer to the English language, specifically the Middle English in which Julian wrote.</note>.

We promise the reader in its perusal a rich treat, both intellectual and spiritual.

->I. T. HECKER.<- <note>Isaac Thomas Hecker (1819–1888) was a prominent American priest and the founder of the Paulist Fathers. He was a key figure in 19th-century American religious thought and sought to bridge the gap between Catholicism and American culture.</note>

NEW YORK, September, 1863.

<meta>This page transitions from the 19th-century preface by Isaac Hecker to the original 17th-century dedication written by the first editor of Julian’s work, Serenus Cressy. It establishes the historical context of the 1670 edition which Hecker reprinted.</meta>

## TO HIS MOST HONOURED LADY, THE LADY MARY BLOUNT OF SODINGTON <note>Mary Blount was the wife of Sir George Blount, a member of a prominent Catholic family in England. During the 17th century, when this was written, Catholic families often supported the secret publication of religious texts.</note>.

MADAM,

THE just and grateful resentment <note>In the 17th century, "resentment" meant a strong feeling or a deep sense of appreciation, rather than the modern meaning of bitterness.</note> which I have of the unmerited kindness, and friendship of your late most worthy and noble husband Sir George Blount, of your Ladyship, and your whole family, obliged me, impatiently to desire an occasion, to make a public acknowledgement thereof. Permit me therefore here, to offer to your Ladyship this small present, in which notwithstanding I can challenge no interest or right, but only the care of publishing it. The author of it <note>This refers to Julian of Norwich, the 14th-century mystic.</note> is a person of your own sex, who lived about three hundred years since <note>The editor, Serenus Cressy, was writing in 1670, roughly 300 years after Julian of Norwich experienced her visions in 1373.</note>, intended it for you, and for such readers as your self, who will not be induced to the perusing of it by curiosity, or a desire to learn strange things, which afterward they will at best vainly admire, or perhaps out of incredulity <term>incredulity: an inability or unwillingness to believe something</term> contemn <term>contemn: to treat with contempt or scorn</term>.

-><insert>BQT</insert><-
-><insert>2416</insert><-
-><insert>.J 8</insert><-
<note>These alphanumeric codes are handwritten library call numbers used to categorize the book for cataloging.</note>

<summary>This is the formal dedication of the 1670 edition of Julian of Norwich's "Revelations of Divine Love," written by Serenus Cressy to Lady Mary Blount. Cressy presents the book as a gesture of gratitude to the Blount family and notes that Julian's 14th-century writings are intended for devout readers rather than the merely curious.</summary>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>
<header>THE EPISTLE DEDICATORY.</header>

But I am certain that your Ladyship will give her <note>Cressy refers to Julian of Norwich or her book as "her."</note> a place in your private prayer room <note>original: "closet"; in the 17th century, a closet was a small, private room intended for prayer, study, or meditation.</note>. There, during your holy moments of solitude <note>original: "devout retirements"</note>, you will enjoy her saintly company <note>original: "conversation"; in this period, the word meant "spiritual intimacy" or "manner of living" as much as spoken words.</note>. You will listen to her as she tells you, with humility and joy, of the miracles of our Lord’s love for her and of the grace He worked within her. By engaging with the book in this way, I have no doubt that you will feel many rays of her spiritual insight and the great warmth of her love <term>charity: the theological term for the highest form of love, signifying the reciprocal love between God and humanity</term> reflecting into your own soul.

Now, that such may be the effects of this book is the sincere desire of,

->Madam,<-
->Your Ladyship’s most humble<-
->and most devoted servant<-
->in our Lord,<-
->H. CRESSY.<- <note>Hugh Paulinus (Serenus) Cressy (c. 1605–1674) was a convert to Catholicism and a Benedictine monk who published the first printed edition of Julian’s work in 1670.</note>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page follows the dedicatory epistle to Lady Mary Blount and serves as a general preface to the reader. It provides historical context regarding the publication's sponsorship by the Abbot of Lambspring and the editor’s decision to preserve the original medieval style of Julian of Norwich’s writing.</meta>

# TO THE READER.
---

**Devout Reader,**

Whatever benefit you may gather from this book, you are indebted for it to a most venerable Abbot <note>The Very Reverend Father John Gascoigne (1600–1681), Lord Abbot of the English Benedictine monastery at Lambspring in Germany.</note> of our nation, by whose command and generosity <note>original: "liberality"</note> it is now published, and consequently, it carries his full approval.

I believed it would have been a disadvantage to the pleasing simplicity of the style to have changed its outward form into our modern language, as some people advised me to do. Yet, I thought it proper to explain certain words or phrases that are no longer in common use in the margin <margin>In this edition, these explanations have been removed from the margins and collected into a Glossary at the end of the volume.</margin>.

I was very interested in telling you something about the blessed <note>original: "happy"; in this context, it implies one who is spiritually blessed or "of happy memory."</note> virgin who compiled these Revelations <note>referring to Julian of Norwich (c. 1342 – c. 1416), the English anchorite and mystic.</note>. However, after all the searching I could do, I could not discover anything regarding her life beyond what she occasionally mentions throughout this book.

<summary>In this preface, the editor explains that the book was published through the patronage of Abbot John Gascoigne and justifies the decision to keep Julian of Norwich's original medieval style rather than modernizing it.</summary>
<keywords>Serenus Cressy, John Gascoigne, Abbot of Lambspring, Julian of Norwich, spiritual literature, 17th-century editing, English Benedictines</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, shifting from the physical production of the book to the biographical details of the author, Julian of Norwich, and the specific historical context of her visions.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

...it itself. The postscript informs us of her name, Juliana, as well as her vocation, which was of the most rigorous type of solitary life <note>An anchorite; a person who lived in total seclusion, usually in a cell attached to a church, to focus entirely on prayer.</note>; she was enclosed her whole life (alone) within four walls. Through this, although all mortal people were shut out from her home, yet saints, angels, and the Supreme King of both could and did find entrance there. Furthermore, in that same postscript, we find that the place greatly honored by her residence and by the visits of her heavenly guest was the City of Norwich.

The time when she lived—and specifically when these heavenly Revelations were granted to her—she herself informs us at the start of the book, was in the year of grace 1373 <note>original: "MCCCLXXIII"</note>. This was about three years before the death of the famous conqueror, King Edward the Third, at which time she was approximately thirty years old. To conclude, in the final chapter of the book, she indicates that more than fifteen years after these Revelations were shown to her, our Lord was pleased to answer her internally through <term>ghostly understanding</term> <note>A technical term in mystical theology referring to spiritual or mental perception, as opposed to a physical sight or an audible voice.</note> to resolve a certain doubt she had regarding the meaning of one of them.

As for the nature of these Revelations, it was the same kind of which we read countless examples among both ancient and modern saints. The subjects of some of them were

<summary>The editor provides biographical details about Julian of Norwich, identifying her as a 14th-century anchorite from Norwich who received her visions in 1373 at the age of thirty.</summary>
<keywords>Julian of Norwich, anchorite, Norwich, 1373, Edward III, ghostly understanding, mysticism, English history</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface, categorizing the types of spiritual visions Julian experienced and explaining her motivation for seeking a near-death experience.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

...represented to her imagination, and perhaps also to her physical sight; sometimes they appeared while she slept, but most often while she was awake. But those visions that were purer, more internal <note>original: "intime"; referring to the innermost depths of the soul</note>, and even more certain, were produced by a divine <term>illapse</term> <note>A theological term for the gentle flowing of the Holy Spirit into the soul.</note> into the spiritual part of her soul—her mind and understanding. This is a process the devil cannot fake, nor can the person receiving it <note>original: "the patient"; here meaning the person experiencing the divine action</note> fully grasp it, yet it removed all doubt or suspicion that she was being deceived.

However, the main point I wish to bring to the reader's attention is the circumstances that came before, and the results that followed, these divine favors granted by Almighty God to his humble and devoted servant.

She was far from expecting or wanting such unusual supernatural gifts. This was her situation: she believed she was still too attached to worldly things <note>original: "unmortified in her affection to creatures"; "mortification" was the practice of subduing physical or worldly desires to focus on the spiritual.</note> and not sensitive enough to our Lord’s love for her. Therefore, to cure the first issue, she prayed for a severe illness—one so extreme that she and everyone else would believe she was dying <note>original: "in her own and others conceit"</note>. She asked for a sickness full of sharp pain and suffering that would strip away all physical comforts and all inner emotional pleasures that might affect the <term>sensual portion</term> <note>The part of the soul related to physical feelings and emotions, rather than pure intellect.</note> of the soul. To remedy the second issue, she begged our Lord to imprint on her soul—in whatever way He saw fit—a

<summary>The editor describes the different levels of Julian's visions, emphasizing that the most profound occurred directly within her mind. He explains that Julian did not seek visions, but rather a near-fatal illness to purge her worldly attachments.</summary>
<keywords>visions, divine illapse, mortification, sensual soul, illness, spiritual preparation</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface, explaining that Julian’s desire for a near-death illness was motivated by a wish to empathize with Christ's suffering and to detach herself from worldly concerns.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

...deep and powerful understanding, and a profound <term>feeling</term> <note>original: "resentment"; in the 17th century, this meant a deep appreciation or sensitive reflection of an experience, rather than bitterness.</note> for those most violent sufferings which He, in His infinite love, endured for her on the cross, so that she might be compelled to return a fitting love to Him.

Yet in making these requests, she expressed a complete surrender to His heavenly will regarding the manner in which they would be granted. The only graces that she did desire—and which we also may and should desire absolutely, without any condition—were a true spiritual hatred and contempt of her selfish nature, and of all worldly or physical <term>pleasures</term> <note>original: "contentments"</note>; a sincere sorrow and <term>remorse</term> <note>original: "compunction"</note> for past sins; and a heartfelt love and reverent fear of Almighty God. These were the gifts she desired. As for the means of obtaining these graces, she suggested what seemed best to her; yet, being certain that God knew what was best for her, she left the method to His Divine pleasure.

It was, undoubtedly, by Divine inspiration that she first made such requests, both in their substance and their manner. Therefore, God granted them as she desired—indeed, in a way more extraordinary than she dared to <term>claim</term> <note>original: "pretend to"; meaning to aspire to or expect.</note>, as the reader may observe. How wonderful the effects of these gifts were will be excellently shown by the whole <term>structure</term> <note>original: "contexture"; the interconnected weaving of her thoughts and narrative.</note> of her writings following each Revelation.

And now, since she herself declares that the <term>insights</term> <note>original: "lights"</note> and

<summary>The editor explains that Julian sought spiritual growth through suffering and self-contempt, ultimately leaving the method of her transformation to God's will. This resulted in extraordinary divine revelations that exceeded her expectations.</summary>
<keywords>divine inspiration, compunction, spiritual growth, resignation to God's will, Julian of Norwich, suffering of Christ</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface, transitioning from the description of Julian’s specific revelations to the universal purpose of her book: the spiritual benefit of all readers.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

...torches <note>original: "torches"; metaphors for the spiritual insights and divine illuminations Julian received.</note> which God was pleased to give her, were intended not for herself alone, but for the universality of God's true servants, for whose benefit also she wrote them. Because of this, the devout reader will, I hope, feel obligated not to be satisfied with mere fruitless admiration, but will, following her example, aspire to a similarly <term>affectuous</term> <note>meaning characterized by deep emotion or "affective" devotion, where the heart is moved toward God.</note> and active contemplation of the total nothingness of creatures <note>A common mystical theme referring to the insignificance of created things when compared to the infinite nature of the Creator.</note>, of the inconceivable ugliness of sin, of the infinite tenderness and <term>indefectibility</term> <note>The quality of being unfailing and constant; God's love is secure and cannot be lost or diminished.</note> of God's love to His <term>elect</term> <note>A theological term referring to those chosen by God for salvation and a life of grace.</note>, and of the <term>omnipotency</term> <note>The state of being all-powerful.</note> of <term>Divine grace</term> <note>The unmerited assistance given by God to humans for their spiritual growth and well-being.</note> working in them; to which grace alone all good in us is to be ascribed.

<summary>This page concludes a section of the preface, explaining that Julian's revelations were meant for the spiritual profit of all Christians, encouraging the reader to seek a deep, active love for God through her example. It emphasizes that all human goodness originates solely from God's all-powerful grace.</summary>
<keywords>Julian of Norwich, spiritual illumination, Divine Grace, the elect, divine love, sin, mystical theology</keywords>

# XVI. REVELATIONS
->OF<-
## LOVE.

---

### *Here beginneth the First Chapter.*

THIS is a Revelation of Love, which Jesus <note>original: "Jesu"</note> Christ, our endless bliss, made in sixteen <term>showings</term> <note>original: "shewings"; these are the specific mystical visions or revelations Julian experienced in May 1373</note>: of which,

The *first* is of his precious crowning of thorns, and therein was contained and specified the blessed Trinity, with the <term>incarnation</term> <note>the Christian doctrine of God becoming human in the person of Jesus</note> and the uniting between God and man's soul, with many fair showings and teachings of endless wisdom and love: in which all the showings that follow are grounded and joined.

The *second* is of the discoloration <note>original: "discolouring"</note> of his fair face, as a sign <note>original: "tokening"</note> of his dear, worthy <term>passion</term> <note>the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus during his crucifixion</note>.

The *third* is, that our Lord God Almighty, all wisdom and all love, just as truly as he has made all things

<summary>This page introduces the "Sixteen Showings" of Julian of Norwich, detailing the first two visions regarding the Crowning of Thorns and the changing appearance of Christ's face during his suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Julian of Norwich, Revelations of Divine Love, Trinity, Incarnation, Passion of Christ, mystical visions, showings</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the sixteen "showings" or mystical visions received by Julian of Norwich, picking up mid-sentence from the third revelation concerning God's providence.</meta>

## SIXTEEN REVELATIONS

that are right, also truly he does and works all things that are done.

The *fourth* is, the scourging of his tender body with plentiful shedding of his precious blood.

The *fifth* is, that the fiend <note>the devil</note> is overcome by the precious <term>passion</term> <note>the suffering of Christ on the cross</note> of Christ.

The *sixth* is, the reverent <note>original: "worshipfull"</note> thanking of our Lord God, in which he rewards all his blessed servants in heaven.

The *seventh* is, the frequent feeling of well-being and woe. The feeling of well-being is a gracious touching and enlightening <note>original: "lightning"</note>, with true <term>certainty</term> <note>original: "sikernes"; a state of spiritual security or absolute confidence in God's promise</note> of endless joy; the feeling of woe is of temptation by the heaviness and weariness of our physical living, with <term>spiritual understanding</term> <note>original: "ghostelie understanding"; knowledge granted by the Holy Spirit rather than human intellect</note> that we are kept just as truly in love, in woe as in well-being, by the goodness of God.

The *eighth* is, the last pains of Christ and his cruel dying.

The *ninth* is, of the pleasure <note>original: "liking"</note> which is in the blessed Trinity from the hard passion of Christ after his pitiful and sorrowful dying; in which joy and pleasure he wills that we be in comfort and mirth with him, until we come to the glory in heaven.

The *tenth* is, our Lord Jesus Christ, through love, having his blessed heart even split in two <note>original: "cloven in two"</note>.

The *eleventh* is, a high spiritual showing of his dear, worthy Mother <note>the Virgin Mary</note>.

The *twelfth* is, that our Lord God is the highest sovereign Being.

# OF DIVINE LOVE.

The *thirteenth* is, that our Lord God wills that we have great regard for all the deeds which he has done in the great nobility of the <term>making</term> <note>creation</note> of all things, and of the excellence of humanity's creation, which is above all his works; and of the precious amends that he has made for human sin, turning all our blame into endless <term>worship</term> <note>honor or glory</note>. *Then he means this: 'Behold and see, for by the same power <note>original: "might"</note>, wisdom, and goodness that I have done all this, by the same power, wisdom, and goodness I shall make well all that is not well, and you shall see it.'* And in this, he wills that we keep ourselves in the faith and truth of the Holy Church, not wishing to know his <term>privities</term> <note>divine secrets or mysteries not meant for human knowledge</note>, except as it belongs to us in this life.

The *fourteenth* is, that our Lord God is the ground of our <term>besekinge</term> <note>beseeching; the act of urgent, humble petitionary prayer</note>. In this were seen two fair properties: the *one* is rightful prayer; the *other* is true trust, which he wills to be equally large <note>meaning the trust should be as great as the prayer</note>, and thus our prayer pleases him, and he out of his goodness fulfills it.

The *fifteenth* is, that we should <term>soudeinlie</term> <note>suddenly</note> be taken from all our pain, and from all our woe, and by his goodness we shall come up above, where we shall have our Lord Jesus as our <term>meed</term> <note>reward or spiritual payment</note>, and to be filled with joy and bliss in heaven.

The *sixteenth* is, that the blessed Trinity, our maker in Christ Jesus our Savior, endlessly dwells in our soul, honorably rewarding and commanding all things, powerfully and wisely saving and keeping us for love, and we shall not be overcome by our enemy <note>the devil</note>.

### The Second Chapter.

<term>This Revelation</term> was made to a simple, unlettered creature, living in <term>deadly flesh</term> <note>original: "deadlie flesh"; refers to the mortal human body subject to death</note>, in the year of our Lord 1373, on the 14th day of May. This creature had previously desired three gifts by the grace of God.

The *first* was a constant <term>mind</term> <note>a meditative focus or deep remembrance</note> of the <term>passion</term> <note>the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus during his crucifixion</note>.
The *second* was a physical sickness.
The *third* was to have, as a gift from God, three "wounds."

As for the first, I thought that I already had some degree of feeling for the passion of Christ, but I still desired to have more by the grace of God. I thought that I would have liked to have been there at that time with Mary <term>Magdalen</term> <note>Mary Magdalene, who in medieval tradition was the model of the "contemplative" and was present at the crucifixion</note> and with others who were Christ's lovers, so that I might have seen physically the passion that our Lord suffered for me. I wanted to have suffered with him as others did who loved him. Therefore, I desired a physical vision in which I might have more knowledge of the bodily pains of our Savior, and of the <term>compassion</term> <note>from the Latin "compati," meaning to suffer with</note> of our Lady and of all his true lovers who were living at that time and saw his pains; for I wished to have been one of them and to have suffered with them. I never desired any other vision or <term>showing</term> <note>revelation</note> from God until the soul had departed from the body, for I believed I would be saved by the mercy of God. This was my intention: that because of that showing, I would afterwards have a more true remembrance of the passion of

For the second [gift] came to my mind with free <term>contrition</term> <note>original: "contrition"; a state of sincere remorse and repentance for sins</note>, without any seeking: a purposeful desire to receive as a gift from God a <term>bodily sickness</term> <note>in the Middle Ages, illness was often viewed as a "purgatory on earth" that could purify the soul</note>. SIXTEEN REVELATIONS

Holy Church, I conceived a powerful desire to receive *three wounds during my life*: that is to say, the wound of *true contrition* <note>original: "verie contrition"; a state of sincere remorse and repentance for sins</note>, the wound of *natural compassion* <note>original: "kind compassion"; in this context, "kind" refers to the natural empathy a person should feel for Christ's suffering</note>, and the wound of *steadfast longing for God*.

Just as I had asked for the first two with a certain condition, I asked for this third one powerfully and without any condition at all. Those two previous desires passed from my mind, but the third remained with me continually.

### Chapter Three.

AND when I was thirty and a half years old, God sent me a physical illness. I lay sick for three days and three nights, and on the fourth night, I received all the <term>Rites of Holy Church</term> <note>original: "Rights of Holie Church"; the sacraments administered to those believed to be near death, including confession and anointing</note>, for I did not expect to live until morning. After this, I lay for two more days and nights. On the third night, I often <term>weened</term> <note>believed or expected</note> that I was passing away, and those who were with me believed the same. 

Even so, in this moment I felt a great reluctance to die—not because of anything on earth that I wanted to live for, nor because of any pain I feared, for I trusted in God’s mercy. Instead, it was because I wanted to live longer so that I might love God better and for a greater length of time, so that through the grace of that life, I might have a deeper knowledge and love of God in the <term>bliss of heaven</term> <note>the state of eternal joy and union with God</note>. For it seemed to me that all the time I had lived

<meta>This page continues the account of Julian's illness. Having expressed a desire to live longer to love God more, she now faces the physical reality of her approaching death and the spiritual transition that begins with the darkening of her physical sight.</meta>

-># OF DIVINE LOVE.<-

...here, so little and so short in regard to that endless bliss, I thought: "Good Lord, may my living be no longer to your worship?" <note>Julian is asking if her life could be extended so she might continue to honor and serve God on earth for a longer time.</note>

And I understood by my reason, and by the feeling of my pains, that I should die; and I assented fully with all the will of my heart to be at God’s will.

Thus I endured until daybreak, and by then my body was dead from the middle downward, as far as I could feel.

Then I was helped to be set upright, propped up with support, so that I might have more freedom in my heart to be at God’s will, and to think on God while my life lasted.

My <term>Curate</term> <note>the local parish priest responsible for the care of souls</note> was sent for to be at my ending; and before he came, I had fixed my eyes <note>original: "eien"</note> upward and could not speak. He set the cross before my face and said: "I have brought the image of our Savior; look upon it and comfort yourself with it." I thought I was well enough as I was, for my eyes were fixed straight up toward heaven, where I trusted to come by the mercy of God.

But nevertheless, I agreed to fix my eyes on the face of the <term>crucifix</term> <note>a physical image of Jesus on the cross</note> if I could, and so I did; for I thought I might endure longer looking straight ahead than looking directly up. After this, my sight began to fail, and it grew as dark around me in the chamber as if it had been night, except for the image of the cross, where a steady light remained—though I did not know how. Everything else besides the cross was ugly and terrifying to me, as if the room were crowded with <term>fiends</term> <note>demons or malevolent spirits</note>.

After this, the upper part <term>over-parte</term> <note>the torso, head, and arms</note> of my body began to die, to such an extent that I scarcely <term>unneath</term> <note>hardly or with difficulty</note> had any feeling; my greatest pain was shortness of breath and the failing of my life.

Then I truly <term>verilie</term> expected to have passed away; and in this state, suddenly <term>sodenlie</term> all my pain was taken from me, and I was as healthy—especially in the upper part of my body—as I ever was before. I marveled at this sudden change, for it seemed to me that it was a secret <term>privie</term> <note>hidden or divine</note> working of God, and not of nature <term>kind</term> <note>referring to "kind" as the natural order of the body</note>. And yet, by feeling this ease, I did not trust any more that I would live; nor was the feeling of this ease a full comfort to me, for I thought I would rather have been delivered from this world, as my heart was willfully <term>wilfullie</term> <note>with strong, purposeful intent</note> set upon that.

Then it suddenly came to my mind that I should desire the second wound <term>seconde wound</term> <note>referring to Julian's earlier prayer for three "wounds": contrition, compassion, and a purposeful longing for God</note> of our Lord’s gift and of His grace: that my body might be filled with the memory and feeling of His blessed Passion <note>the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus</note>, as I had prayed for before. For I wished that His pains might be my pains, through compassion, followed by a longing for God.

Thus it seemed to me that I might, by His grace, receive the wounds that I had previously desired. But in this, I never desired any physical vision <term>bodilie sight</term>, nor any kind of "showing" from God—only compassion, such as I thought a natural <term>kind</term> soul might have for our Lord Jesus, who for love chose to become a mortal <term>deadlie</term> <note>subject to death; human</note> man. I desired to suffer while living in my mortal body, as God would give me grace.

# The First Revelation.

## *The Fourth Chapter.*

**AND** in this moment, suddenly I saw the red blood running down from under the crown <term>garland</term> <note>While we usually say "crown of thorns," Julian uses the word "garland," which suggests a circular wreath.</note>, hot and fresh, abundant and life-like, exactly as it was at the time that the crown of thorns was pressed on his blessed head—the same one, both God and man, who suffered for me. And in that same revelation, suddenly the Trinity filled my heart with the greatest joy; and I understood that it shall be this way in heaven forever for all who shall come there. For the Trinity is God; God is the Trinity. The Trinity is our Creator <term>Maker</term>, the Trinity is our Protector <term>Keeper</term>, the Trinity is our everlasting Lover, the Trinity is our endless joy and our bliss, through our Lord Jesus Christ and in our Lord Jesus Christ. And this was shown in the first vision, and in all of them; for whenever Jesus appears, the blessed Trinity is understood, according to my sight.

## *The Fifth Chapter.*

At the same time that I saw this vision of the head bleeding, our good Lord showed a spiritual <term>ghostly</term> vision of his intimate <term>homely</term> <note>In Julian’s writing, "homely" signifies a deep, comfortable intimacy and friendship between God and the soul, rather than meaning "plain" or "unattractive."</note> love: I saw that he is everything to us that is good and comforting for our help.

> ‘ *It is everything that is created.* ’

For this is the reason why we are not all at ease in heart and soul: because we seek rest here in this thing that is so little, where no rest is to be found; and we do not know our God who is all-mighty, all-wise, and all-good, for he is true <term>verie</term> rest.

God wants to be known, and it pleases him that we rest ourselves in him.

For everything that is beneath him is not enough for us.

## *The Sixth Chapter.*

THIS revelation <term>shewing</term> was given to my understanding to teach our soul to wisely cling <term>cleave</term> to the **goodness** of God, and at that same time the custom of our prayer was brought to my mind: how we are used to using many intermediaries <term>meanes</term> <note>Julian observes that people often use "means"—such as the intercession of saints or specific religious rituals—to reach God because they do not yet fully understand the directness of His love.</note>, because we do not know love. ->THE FIRST REVELATION.<-

faithfully pray to him for his goodness, and cling to it by his grace with true understanding and steadfast belief, than if we utilized all the means <term>intermediaries</term> <note>Julian uses "means" to refer to the various helpers or channels of grace—like saints or rituals—that assist a believer in reaching God.</note> that the heart can imagine.

For if we use all these means, it is too little, and not full honor <term>worship</term> to God; but in his goodness is everything as a whole, and there nothing is lacking.

For this, as I shall say, came to my mind at the same time: we pray to God for his holy flesh, and for his precious blood, his holy suffering <term>passion</term>, his dear, worthy death, and honorable <term>worshipful</term> wounds; for all the blessed nature <term>kindness</term> <note>Here, "kindness" refers to "kind" or "nature"—specifically the natural bond and kinship created between God and humanity through Christ.</note> and the endless life that we have from all this, it is from the goodness of God. And we pray to him for the love of his sweet mother who bore him; and all the help that we receive from her, it is from his goodness. And we pray for his holy cross that he died on; and all the help and all the power <term>virtue</term> <note>In the medieval sense, "virtue" refers to the inherent strength or spiritual efficacy of a holy object or act.</note> that we have from that cross, it is from his goodness.

And in the same way, all the help that we have from particular <term>special</term> saints, and from all the blessed company of heaven; the dear, worthy love and the holy, endless friendship that we have from them, it is from his goodness.

For the means that the goodness of God has ordained to help us are very beautiful <term>fair</term> and many.

Of which the chief and principal means is the blessed human nature <term>kind</term> <note>Julian refers to the Incarnation—the "kind" or nature of humanity that Jesus took on when he was born of the Virgin Mary.</note> that he took from the maiden, with all the means that went before, and come after, which belong to our redemption and to our endless salvation.

<summary>Julian explains that while holy aids like the Virgin Mary, the saints, and the Cross are vital, they are "means" that derive all their power from the primary source: God’s own goodness. She emphasizes that the greatest of these means is the Incarnation, where Christ took on human nature.</summary>

Therefore it pleases Him that we seek Him and worship Him through means <term>intermediaries</term> <note>Julian refers to the various spiritual channels—like prayer, sacraments, or saints—that lead the believer to God.</note>, understanding and knowing that He is the goodness of everything.

For the goodness of God is the highest form of prayer, and it comes down to us at the lowest part of our need. It is nearest to us by nature <term>kind</term> <note>Julian frequently uses "kind" to mean "nature" or "natural," emphasizing that God is the very origin and essence of our being.</note> and most ready to help in grace.

A person walks upright, and the internal parts <term>soul of his body</term> <note>While the text reads "soul," Julian is using a physiological metaphor here; she is describing the internal organs and the digestive process to show that no part of human existence is beneath God's notice.</note> are closed <term>sparred</term> as a beautiful purse; and when the time of necessity comes, it is opened and then closed again in a very decent <term>honestly</term> <note>In Middle English, "honestly" implies something done properly, fittingly, or with dignity.</note> manner.

For just as the body is clothed in cloth, and the flesh in skin, and the bones in flesh, and the heart in the chest <term>bulke</term>, so are we—soul and body—clothed and enclosed in the goodness of God. ## *The Eighth Chapter.*

AND as long as I saw this sight of the abundance <term>plentuousness</term> of bleeding from the head, I could never stop repeating these words: Bless the Lord <note>original: "Benedicite Dominus"</note>. Within this revelation, I understood six things.

The *first* is the signs <term>tokens</term> of the blessed suffering <term>passion</term> and the abundant shedding of his precious blood.

The *second* is the Virgin <term>maiden</term> who is his dear, worthy mother.

The *third* is the blessed Divinity <term>God-head</term> that ever was and shall be: all-mighty, all-Wisdom, and all-Love.

The *fourth* is everything that he has made; for I know well that heaven and earth, and all that is created, is great, vast, beautiful, and good. But the reason it appeared so small in my sight was because I saw it in the presence of him who is the Creator <term>Maker</term>; for to a soul that sees the Creator of all things, all that is created seems very small.

And I said this because I expected <term>went</term> to have died; for on the day that a man or woman dies, they are judged individually <term>deemed particularly</term>, as they shall remain without end, according to my understanding.

I said this because I wanted them to love God better, and to remind them that this life is short, as they could see by my example; for during this entire time I thought <term>weened</term> I was dying, and that was a marvel <term>marvaile</term> to me and a partial wonder. For it seemed to me that this vision was shown for those who would continue to live. Everything I say about myself, I mean on behalf of <term>in person of</term> all my fellow Christians; for I have been taught through the spiritual revelation of our Lord God that he intends it so.

### *The Ninth Chapter.*

**BECAUSE** of this revelation <term>shewing</term> I am not "good" in myself; I am only good if I love God better, and in so far as you love God better, the revelation is more for you than it is for me.

I do not say this to those who are wise, for they know it well; but I say it to those of you who are simple, for your ease and comfort. For we are all one in love. Truly, this was not revealed to me because God loves me better than the humblest soul who is in a state of grace; for I am certain there are many who never had a revelation or vision <term>sight</term> other than the common teaching of the Holy Church, who yet love God better than I do. For if I look at myself individually <term>singularly</term>, I am absolutely nothing; but in general, I am, I hope, in the unity <term>one-head</term> of charity with all my fellow Christians <term>even Christian</term> <note>Julian’s term "even Christian" means "fellow Christian," highlighting the spiritual equality and shared humanity of all believers.</note>. For in this unity <term>one-head</term> stands the life of all mankind who shall be saved; for God is everything that is good, as I see it.

<summary>Julian humbly insists that her visions do not make her superior to others and emphasizes that her revelations are intended for the collective comfort and spiritual benefit of all "even Christians." And God has made everything that is made, and God loves all that He has made; and whoever generally loves all their fellow Christians <term>even Christen</term> <note>Julian’s term "even Christen" means "fellow Christian," highlighting the spiritual equality and shared humanity of all believers.</note> for God's sake, loves everything that exists.

I intended never to accept anything that might be contrary to it. # The Second Revelation.

## *The Tenth Chapter.*

AND after this, I saw with physical vision <term>bodily sight</term> in the face of the crucifix that hung before me: in which I continually beheld a part of His passion; contempt, spitting, soiling <term>sulloing</term> <note>Julian uses "sulloing" (related to the modern "sullying") to describe the defilement and staining of Christ’s face during His torture.</note>, and buffeting, and many lingering <term>languring</term> pains, more than I can tell; and frequent changing of color. And at one time I saw how half the face, beginning at the ear, was covered over <term>over yede</term> <note>Meaning "went over" or "spread over."</note> with dry blood until it reached the middle of the face <term>mid-face</term>; and after that, the other half was covered in the same way. And meanwhile, it vanished on that side even as it had appeared.

This I saw by physical vision, both as a revelation and yet obscurely; and I desired more physical light to have seen it more clearly, and I was answered in my reason: ‘*If God wishes to show you more, He shall be your light; you need none but Him.*’

For I saw Him and I sought Him: for we are now so blind and so unwise that we can never seek God until the time that He, out of His goodness, shows Himself to us. And when we graciously see anything of Him, then we are stirred by that same grace to seek with great desire to see Him even more blessedly. And thus I saw Him and I sought Him.

<summary>In her second revelation, Julian witnesses a vivid physical vision of Christ's suffering on the cross, specifically the discoloration and bleeding of His face. She reflects on the spiritual necessity of God initiating contact with the soul, noting that humans only seek God because He has first revealed Himself through grace.</summary>

->THE SECOND REVELATION.<-

...and I possessed Him and I lacked Him <note>original: "wanted him". In Julian's English, "wanting" meant lacking or needing something not yet fully possessed.</note>: and this is, and should be, our common work in this life, as I see it.

At one time my understanding was let down to the bottom of the sea <term>sea-ground</term>; and there I saw hills and green valleys, appearing as if they were overgrown with seaweed <term>wrake</term> and gravel. Then I understood this: that if a man or woman were there, deep under the wide water, and if he could have a sight of God—as God is with a person continually—he would be safe in soul and body and take no harm. Moreover, he would have more solace and comfort than all this world can ever tell.

For He wills that we believe that we see Him continually, even though it seems to us that it is but little; and through this belief, He makes us evermore to receive grace. For He will be seen, and He will be sought, and He will be waited for <term>abiden</term>, and He will be trusted.

This second revelation <term>shewing</term> <note>Julian uses "shewing" as her standard term for these divine visions.</note> was so lowly, and so small, and so simple, that my spirits were in great distress <term>travail</term> in the beholding—mourning, fearful, and longing. It was a figure and a likeness of our foul, black deeds, which our fair, bright, blessed Lord bore for our sin. It made me think of the **Holy Vernacle** <note>The "Holy Vernacle" (Veronica) refers to the legendary veil used to wipe Christ's face on the way to the Crucifixion, which was said to have miraculously retained the image of His bloodied features. It was a famous relic in Rome during the Middle Ages.</note> of Rome, which He portrayed with His own blessed face when He was in His harsh passion, willingly going to His death, and often...

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues Julian’s meditation on the Second Revelation, specifically the "Holy Vernacle" (the veil of Veronica). On the previous page, she described seeing a vision of Christ's face that was discolored and reflected the "foul, black deeds" of humanity. Here, she explores the theological paradox of how the most beautiful face in heaven could become so marred by suffering.</meta>

->THE SECOND REVELATION.<-

...changing of color: the brown head and the black head, the rueful face <term>rewlyhead</term> and the gaunt face <term>lean-head</term>.

Many people wondered about this image <note>The image of Christ's face on the "Holy Vernacle" or Veronica's veil, which according to tradition, was miraculously imprinted with His blood and sweat.</note> and how it could remain as it did; for he portrayed <term>protraid</term> it with his blessed face, which is the beauty of heaven, the flower of the earth, and the fruit of the Maiden’s <note>the Virgin Mary’s</note> womb. How then could this image be so discolored and so far from beauty <term>fair-head</term>? I desire to see this, as I have understood it by the grace of God.

We know in our faith and in our belief, through the teaching and preaching of Holy Church, that the blessed Trinity made mankind in his image and likeness. In the same way, we know that when man fell so deeply and so wretchedly through sin, there was no other help to restore man except through him who created man.

And he who made man out of love, by that same love he willed to restore man to the same bliss—and a bliss even greater <note>original: "over-passing", meaning a state of grace that exceeds what was lost in Eden.</note>.

And just as we were made like the Trinity in our first creation, our Maker willed that we should be like Jesus Christ our Savior in heaven without end, by the virtue of our own creation.

Then, between these two points <note>the beginning of creation and our final glory in heaven</note>, he willed—out of love and for the honor of man—to make himself as like to man in this mortal <term>deadly</term> life, in our wretchedness <term>foul-head</term> and in our misery, as a man might be without having any guilt. This is what it means, as was said before: it was the image and the likeness of our foul, black deeds, within which our fair, bright, blessed Lord hid his Divinity <term>God-head</term>.

But truly I dare to say, and we ought to believe, that so...

<summary>Julian reflects on the paradox of Christ's face, explaining how his heavenly beauty was obscured by the "foulness" of human sin during the Passion. She highlights that God became human in all our wretchedness—except for guilt—specifically to restore humanity to a state of eternal bliss.</summary>
<keywords>Image of God, Trinity, Incarnation, Holy Vernacle, Passion of Christ, Divine Love, Redemption, Sin, Human Nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Julian’s meditation on the Second Revelation. ->THE SECOND REVELATION.<-

...no man was ever so beautiful as he, until the time that his fair complexion was changed by labor <term>travaile</term> <note>In Julian’s time, "travaile" referred to both painful physical labor and the spiritual struggle of the Passion.</note> and sorrow, suffering and death.

The Second Revelation speaks of this in the eighteenth chapter, where it describes this likeness further. There it mentions the <term>Vernacle of Rome</term> <note>The "Vernacle" is the Veil of Saint Veronica, a famous relic in Rome believed to have been used to wipe Christ’s face on the way to the crucifixion, leaving a miraculous impression of His features.</note>; it refers to the various changes of color, which sometimes appear more comforting and full of life, and at other times more sorrowful and death-like, as will be seen later. This vision was a lesson for my understanding: that the soul’s constant seeking greatly pleases God, for a soul can do no more than seek, suffer, and trust.

And this work is performed in every soul that has it by the Holy Spirit.

The clarity of finding Him is a matter of special grace, granted when it is His will. Seeking with faith, hope, and love <note>original: "charity", meaning the highest form of Christian love.</note> pleases our Lord; and the finding pleases the soul, filling it with joy.

And thus was my understanding taught that seeking is as good as beholding <note>Julian uses "beholding" to mean a direct, clear vision or spiritual experience of God’s presence.</note>, for as long as He allows the soul to be in labor. It is God’s will that we seek until we reach the beholding of Him; for by that seeking, He shall show Himself to us by His special grace, when He wills.

And how a soul shall conduct herself in His beholding, He shall teach her Himself; and that brings Him the most honor <term>worship</term>, and the soul the most profit, and is most received through humility and virtue, with the grace and leading of the Holy...

<summary>Julian discusses the "Vernacle of Rome" to explain how Christ's appearance fluctuates between life and death in her vision, emphasizing that the act of seeking God is as spiritually valuable as the act of seeing Him.</summary>
<keywords>Julian of Norwich, Second Revelation, Vernacle of Rome, Veronica's Veil, seeking vs. beholding, spiritual labor, Holy Spirit, divine grace</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues Julian’s meditation on the Second Revelation. On the previous page, Julian noted that seeking God is as pleasing to Him as finding Him ("beholding"), and that this spiritual work is guided by the Holy Spirit.</meta>

->THE SECOND REVELATION.<-

...Spirit; for a soul that simply resigns itself to God with true trust, whether in seeking or in beholding, performs the highest worship it can do, in my view.

These are two workings that can be seen in this vision: one is *seeking*, the other is *beholding*. Seeking is common, something every soul can have through His grace, and it should involve the discernment and teaching of the Holy Church.

It is God's will that we have three things in our seeking of His gift. The *first* is that we seek willfully and diligently without <term>sloth</term> <note>Sloth (original: "sloath") or 'accidie' was considered a spiritual apathy or laziness that prevents one from doing good.</note>, as much as we are able through His grace—gladly and merrily, without unreasonable gloom and empty sorrow. The *second* is that we wait for Him steadfastly for the sake of His love, without grumbling or struggling against Him, until our life's end; for this struggle shall last but a short while. The *third* is that we trust in Him powerfully with full and true faith; for it is His will that we know He shall appear suddenly and blissfully to all those who love Him.

For His working is <term>hidden</term> <note>original: "privy"; meaning secret or mysterious, referring to the way God acts within the soul without always being visible.</note>, yet He desires to be perceived; and His appearance shall be sweet and sudden. He wants to be trusted, for He is completely <term>familiar</term> <note>original: "homely"; one of Julian’s most famous terms, describing God's intimate, unpretentious, and domestic closeness to the human soul.</note> and courteous. Blessed may He be.

<summary>Julian outlines the dual nature of spiritual life—seeking and beholding—and provides three requirements for a seeker: diligent effort, patient endurance, and total trust in God’s eventual, joyful appearance.</summary>
<keywords>seeking, beholding, Holy Church, sloth, spiritual diligence, steadfastness, homely, Julian of Norwich</keywords>

->THE THIRD REVELATION.<-

# The Third Revelation.

## *The Eleventh Chapter.*

**AND** after this, I saw God in a point <note>original: "in a point"; a metaphysical concept suggesting that God is the center and the whole of existence, present in the smallest indivisible unit of being.</note>; that is to say, in my understanding: by which sight I saw that He is in all things. I beheld with careful attention, seeing and knowing in that sight, that He does all that is done. I marveled in that sight with a soft dread <note>A gentle, reverent awe rather than paralyzing fear.</note>, and thought: "what is sin?" For I saw truly that God does all things, be they ever so little. And I saw verily that nothing is done by <term>hap</term> <note>original: "happ"; luck or chance.</note>, nor by <term>adventure</term> <note>original: "adventure"; an accidental occurrence or random event.</note>, but all by the aforesaid wisdom of God. If it seems like luck or chance in the sight of man, our <term>blindness</term> <note>original: "blindhead"; a state of spiritual ignorance or lack of perception.</note> and our lack of foresight is the cause. For those things that are in the aforesaid wisdom of God have been there from without beginning, which rightfully and worthily He continually leads to the best end: as it comes about, falling upon us suddenly, without our knowing. And thus by our blindness and our lack of foresight, we say these things happen by luck and by chance.

Thus I understand in this revelation of love: for well I know that in the sight of our Lord God, there is no luck, nor chance. Therefore, I was necessarily compelled to admit that all things that are done, are well done; for our Lord God does all: for at this time the working of creatures was not shown, but

<summary>Julian begins her Third Revelation, describing a vision of God as the center of all things and concluding that since God performs all actions, nothing happens by chance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Third Revelation, following Julian’s realization that God is the doer of all things. Having established that God is in the "mid-point," she now addresses the problem of sin and the difference between human judgment and divine perspective.</meta>

->THE THIRD REVELATION.<-

...of our Lord God in all created things; for he is in the center of all things, and he performs all that is done. And I am certain that he commits no sin.

And here I saw truly that sin is no deed; for in all of this, sin was not shown. <note>Julian is expressing a famous mystical concept: that sin has no "substance" or "being" of its own. It is a privation or a lack of good, rather than a thing created by God.</note> And I did not want to wonder about this any longer, but instead watched our Lord to see what he would show next.

And so, as much as was possible at that time, the <term>rightfulness</term> <note>original: "rightfulness"; meaning both justice and the quality of being perfectly "right" or straight.</note> of God’s work was revealed to the soul.

*Rightfulness* has two beautiful properties. It is *right*, and it is *full*: and so are all the works of our Lord. And for these works, there is no need for the operation of mercy or grace: for they are all perfectly right, and in them nothing is lacking. And at another time, he showed me sin "naked" <note>Stripped of its disguises, or seen as the empty nothingness she believes it to be.</note>, as I shall describe later. Afterward, when he uses the working of mercy and grace, this vision was revealed to my understanding; for our Lord wants the soul to be truly turned toward the beholding of him, and generally toward all his works, for they are entirely good.

And all his <term>dooms</term> <note>original: "dooms"; judgments or decrees.</note> are easy and sweet. They are like two great eyes bringing the soul—which has turned away from the blind <term>deeming</term> <note>original: "deeming"; human opinion or the act of judging based on limited perception.</note> of man—into the beautiful, sweet judgment of our Lord God. For man considers some deeds to be well done and some deeds to be evil; but our Lord does not see them that way. For just as everything that has existence in <term>kind</term> <note>original: "kind"; nature or the natural world.</note> is of God's making, so is everything that is done, by its very nature, of God's doing. For it is easy to understand that the best deed is well done; and just as well as the best and highest deed is done, so too is the least deed done well...

<summary>Julian reflects on the nature of divine justice ("rightfulness"), arguing that because God performs all deeds, sin has no real existence or "substance." She contrasts the "blind judgment" of humans, who categorize deeds as good or evil, with God’s perspective, in which all actions are perfectly executed.</summary>
<keywords>sin as no deed, rightfulness, divine judgment, dooms, deeming, kind, nature, providence</keywords>

->THE THIRD REVELATION.<-

...deed done: and all according to the nature and the order that our Lord has ordained for it from before the beginning. For there is no <term>Doer</term> <note>original: "Door"; likely a variant spelling or typographical error for "Doer" in the original manuscript, reinforcing Julian's point that God is the ultimate agent of all actions.</note> but he.

I saw quite truly that he never changed his purpose in any kind of thing, nor ever shall, without end; for there was nothing unknown to him in his rightful <term>ordinance</term> <note>original: "ordinance"; God's providential plan or divine decree.</note> from before the beginning. And therefore all things were set in order before anything was made, just as they should stand forever; and no kind of thing shall fail in any point; for he has made all things in the <term>fulhead</term> <note>original: "fulhead"; an archaic term for "fullness," "completeness," or "perfection."</note> of goodness.

And therefore the blessed Trinity is always fully pleased in all his works. And all this he showed most blessedly, meaning this: ‘ *See, I am God. See, I am in all things. See, I do all things. See, I never took my hands off my works, nor ever shall, without end. See, I lead all things to the end that I ordained them for, from before the beginning, by the same power, wisdom, and love that I made them with. How could anything be wrong?* ’ Thus mightily, wisely, and lovingly was the soul examined in this vision. Then I saw truly that I must necessarily agree with great reverence and joy in God.

<summary>Julian concludes her third vision by explaining that God has a perfect, unchanging plan from before time began. She records a divine message asserting that God is in all things and performs all actions, leading the soul to conclude that nothing is "amiss" in the divine order.</summary>

->THE FOURTH REVELATION.<-

## The Fourth Revelation.

### The Twelfth Chapter.

**AND** after this I saw, beholding the body bleeding plentifully, in the appearance of the <term>scourging</term> <note>original: "scourging"; the ritual beating or whipping of Jesus by Roman soldiers prior to his crucifixion.</note>, as follows: the fair skin was broken very deep into the tender flesh, with sharp strikes, all over the sweet body. The hot blood ran out so plentifully that neither skin nor wound could be seen, but only blood. And when it reached the place where it should have fallen down, there it vanished. Nevertheless, the bleeding continued for a while, until it could be seen with <term>advisement</term> <note>original: "advisement"; careful, deliberate observation or concentrated attention.</note>. And this was so plentiful to my sight that I thought if it had been so in <term>kind</term> <note>original: "in kind"; in its physical nature or material substance.</note> and in substance at that time, it would have made the bed all bloody and would have spread everywhere.

Then it came to my mind that God has made waters plentiful on earth for our service and for our physical comfort, out of the tender love He has for us. But yet it pleases Him better that we take, in a most <term>wholesome</term> <note>original: "holsomely"; in a way that promotes spiritual health or healing.</note> way, His blessed blood to wash us from sin; for there is no liquid that has been made that pleases Him so much to give us. For it is most plentiful, just as it is most precious, and that is by the power of the blessed <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "Godhead"; the divine nature or essence of God; the Trinity.</note>; and it is our own <term>nature</term> <note>original: "kind"; Julian often uses this word to signify both "human nature" and the "natural" bond between God and humanity.</note>, and it blessedly overflows us by the power of His precious love.

<summary>Julian describes a vivid vision of Christ’s body during the scourging, where His blood flows so freely that it hides His skin before miraculously vanishing. She concludes that while God provides water for our physical needs, He provides Christ's blood as the ultimate, most precious "liquid" to cleanse human sin.</summary>
<keywords>Fourth Revelation, scourging of Christ, Sacred Blood, atonement, Godhead, divine love, Julian of Norwich, Passion of Christ</keywords>

->THE FOURTH REVELATION.<-

The dear worthy blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, just as it is truly most precious, is also truly most <term>plentiful</term> <note>original: "plentuous"; overflowing or abundant.</note>. It descended down into hell and broke her bonds, and delivered all those who were there who belong to the court of heaven. The precious abundance of his dear worthy blood overflows all the earth and is ready to wash all creatures of sin who are of good-will, have been, or shall be. The precious abundance of his dear worthy blood rises up into heaven in the blessed body of our Lord Jesus Christ: and there he is bleeding, praying for us to the Father, and he is and shall be as long as we have need. And it flows forever throughout all heaven, rejoicing in the salvation of all mankind who are there, and shall be, <term>completing the number that is lacking</term> <note>original: "fulfilling the number that faileth"; referring to the medieval theological idea that human souls were created to fill the empty seats in heaven left by the fallen angels.</note>.

->THE FIFTH REVELATION.<-

3*

Then he, without a voice and without the opening of lips, formed these words in my soul: “*By this, the <term>fiend</term> <note>original: "fiend"; the devil or Satan.</note> is overcome.*” Our Lord spoke this word, referring to his blessed passion, as he showed before. In this, our Lord showed a portion of the fiend's malice, and fully his <term>impotence</term> <note>original: "unmight"; lack of power or total weakness.</note>; for he showed that Christ's passion is the overcoming of the fiend. God showed that the fiend has the same malice now that he had before the <term>Incarnation</term> <note>The theological event of God becoming human in the person of Jesus.</note>; and he <term>labors</term> <note>original: "travelleth"; toils painfully or exerts great effort.</note> just as intensely. For all that God allows him to do turns to joy for us, and to shame and pain for him. And he has just as much sorrow when God gives him leave to work as when he does not work; and that is because he may never do as much evil as he would like, for his power is entirely locked in God’s hand.

But in God, there can be no wrath, as I saw it. For our good Lord, endlessly regarding his own honor and the profit of all those who shall be saved with might and right, withstands the <term>reproved</term> <note>original: "reproved"; those who are rejected or who have turned away from God.</note>, who out of malice and <term>wickedness</term> <note>original: "shrewdness"; in this medieval context, it refers to depravity or malicious intent.</note> busy themselves to oppose and act against God’s will.

Also, I saw our Lord scorning his malice and <term>setting at naught</term> <note>original: "naughting"; treating as nothing or rendering trivial.</note> his impotence, and he wills that we do the same. Because of this sight, I laughed greatly, and that made those who were around me laugh as well, and their laughing was a <term>delight</term> <note>original: "liking"; a source of pleasure or satisfaction.</note> to me. I thought that I wished all my <term>fellow Christians</term> <note>original: "even Christen"; a Middle English term for one's neighbors or peers in the faith.</note> had seen what I saw...

->THE FIFTH REVELATION.<-

If others had seen what I saw, then they would all have laughed with me; but I did not see Christ laughing. However, I know well that the sight he showed me made me laugh: for I understood that we may laugh to comfort ourselves and rejoice in God, because the <term>fiend</term> <note>original: "fiend"; the devil or Satan.</note> is overcome. And there I saw God scorn his malice; it was revealed to my understanding by looking into our Lord; that is to say, it was an inward showing of <term>truth</term> <note>original: "soothfastness"; truth, reality, or faithfulness.</note> without any change in his <term>expression</term> <note>original: "cheere"; facial expression or outward mood.</note>: for, as I saw it, this is an honorable attribute in God which is everlasting.

And after this I fell into a sadness, and said, 

> “I see three things: <term>play</term> <note>original: "game"; a joke, sport, or joyful victory.</note>, scorn, and <term>earnestness</term> <note>original: "earnest"; something serious, weighty, or real.</note>. I see play, in that the fiend is overcome; and I see scorn, in that God scorns him, and he shall be scorned; and I see earnestness, in that he is overcome by the blessed passion and death of our Lord Jesus Christ, which was done in great earnestness and with <term>heavy labor</term> <note>original: "sad travel"; in Middle English, "sad" meant serious or heavy, and "travel" (travail) meant painful toil or labor.</note>.”

And when I said that he is scorned, I meant that God scorns him; that is to say, because He sees him now as He shall see him without end. For in this, God showed that the fiend is damned. And I meant this when I said he *should* be scorned: for I saw that he shall be scorned on <term>Judgment Day</term> <note>original: "doomes-day"; the final judgment of humanity.</note> by all those who shall be saved, toward whose salvation he has felt great envy. For then he shall see that all the woe and tribulation he has caused them shall be turned into an increase of their joy without end. And all the pain and the sorrow that he would have brought them to shall go with him to hell forever.

<summary>Julian describes her vision of the devil's defeat, which causes her to laugh in spiritual triumph, before reflecting on the serious price Christ paid for that victory. ->THE SEVENTH REVELATION.<-

# The Seventh Revelation.

### *The Fifteenth Chapter.*

AND after this, he showed a supreme spiritual <term>spiritual</term> <note>original: "ghostly"; in Julian’s time, "ghostly" referred to the soul or spirit, rather than a phantom.</note> delight <term>delight</term> <note>original: "liking"; a sense of pleasure, enjoyment, or satisfaction.</note> in my soul: in this delight, I was filled with everlasting certainty, powerfully secured without any painful fear. ->THE SEVENTH REVELATION.<-

now one state, and then the other, various times; I suppose about twenty times. And in the time of joy, I might have said with Saint Paul: ‘Nothing shall separate <term>separate</term> <note>original: "depart"; in Middle English, "depart" often meant to separate or divide.</note> me from the love <term>love</term> <note>original: "charity"; refers to *agape*, the divine love of Christ.</note> of Christ.’ And in the pain I might have said with Saint Peter: ‘Lord save me, I perish.’

This vision was shown to teach me in my understanding that it is beneficial <term>beneficial</term> <note>original: "speedful"; meaning helpful, profitable, or advantageous for spiritual progress.</note> for some souls to feel in this way: sometimes to be in comfort, and sometimes to fail and to be left to themselves. Also, I did not deserve to have had this blissful feeling, but our Lord gives it freely when he wills; and he allows us to be in woe sometimes, and both are one and the same love.

For it is God's will that we keep ourselves in comfort with all our might; for bliss is everlasting without end, and pain is passing, and shall be brought to nothing for those who shall be saved. Therefore, it is not God’s will that we follow the feeling of pains by sorrowing and mourning for them, but rather that we pass over them quickly and keep ourselves in the endless delight <term>delight</term> <note>original: "liking"; a state of spiritual joy or satisfaction.</note> that is God.

# The Eighth Revelation.
## And the Sixteenth Chapter.

AFTER this, Christ showed a part of his <term>Passion</term> <note>original: "passion"; this term refers to the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus Christ in the hours leading up to and including his crucifixion.</note>, near the moment of his dying: I saw the sweet face looking dry and bloodless, with a pale dying, and a dead, pale <term>languishing</term> <note>original: "languring"; a state of fading away or weakening from intense suffering.</note>, and then it turned even more deathly into blue, and afterward into a brownish-blue, as the flesh turned more deeply dead. For his suffering showed itself to me most clearly in his blessed face, and specifically in his lips; there I saw these four colors—those that were previously fresh and red, lively and pleasing to my sight. This was a pitiful change, to see this deep dying; and also his skin was <term>shriveling</term> <note>original: "cloggering"; Julian uses this word to describe the way the skin and blood dried, bunched, and congealed together due to extreme dehydration.</note> and dried to my sight.

And at the time when the precious blood was bled out of the sweet body, as much as could pass from it, there was still a moisture in the sweet flesh of Christ, as it was shown. Blood loss and pain dried him from within, and the blowing of the wind and cold coming from without met together in the sweet body of Christ; and

### *The Twenty-first Chapter.*

SUDDENLY, as I was looking at the same cross, his <term>blessedful chear</term> <note>original: "blessedful chear"; a joyful, holy facial expression or countenance.</note> changed. The changing of his expression changed mine, and I became as glad and merry as it was possible to be. Then our Lord merrily brought to my mind: ‘*Where is any part of your pain or your anguish now?*’ And I was completely merry; I understood that we are now, in our Lord’s <term>meaning</term> <note>original: "meaning"; spiritual intention or purpose.</note>, on his cross with him—dying in our own pains and in our own <term>passion</term> <note>original: "passion"; used here to mean individual suffering that mirrors Christ's own.</note>. And if we willingly remain on that same cross, with his help and his grace, until the very last point, he shall suddenly change his expression toward us; and we shall be with him in heaven. Between the one state and the other, all shall seem as one single moment; and then all things shall be brought into joy. And so he meant in this <term>shewing</term> <note>original: "shewing"; a divine revelation or vision.</note>, ‘*Where is any part of your pain or your grief now?*’ 

And we shall be full of bliss; and here I truly saw that if he showed us his joyful expression now, there is no pain on earth, nor in any other place, that could trouble us; instead, all things would be joy and bliss to us. But because he shows us the sad expression of his passion, just as he carried his cross in this life, we therefore experience <term>disease</term> <note>original: "disease"; literally "dis-ease"—discomfort, distress, or lack of peace.</note> and <term>travaile</term> <note>original: "travaile"; hard labor, painful struggle, or exertion.</note> with him, as our <term>kind</term> <note>original: "kind"; human nature.</note> requires. And the reason why he suffers is because he desires, out of his goodness, to make us heirs with him in his bliss.

<summary>Julian describes a sudden transformation where Christ's suffering expression on the cross changes to one of joy, symbolizing the eventual transition of human suffering into heavenly bliss. She concludes that while we must suffer "in our kind" during this life, the joy of God's presence would instantly nullify all earthly pain.</summary>

### *The Twenty-second Chapter.*

THEN our good Lord asked, ‘*Are you well satisfied <note>original: "apaid"; pleased or content.</note> that I suffered for you?*’ I said, ‘Yes, good Lord, <term>many thanks</term> <note>original: "gramercy"; from the French "grand merci," meaning great thanks.</note>; yes, good Lord, blessed may you be.’ Then Jesus our good Lord said, ‘*If you are satisfied, I am satisfied: It is a joy, a bliss, an endless delight to me, that I ever suffered my passion for you: and if I could have suffered more, I would have suffered more.*’ In this feeling my understanding was lifted up into heaven: and there I saw three heavens; I marveled greatly at this sight, and thought, “I see three heavens,” and all were within the <term>blessed humanity</term> <note>original: "blissedful man-head"; referring to the human nature of Christ.</note> of Christ; and none is larger, none is smaller, none is higher, none is lower, but all are equal in bliss. Truly, the number surpassed my understanding and my senses so far that my reason could not—and would not be able to—comprehend or grasp it.

### The Twenty-third Chapter.

AND in these *three* phrases: "*It is a joy, a bliss, and an endless delight to me*," three heavens were revealed. For the joy, I understood the <term>pleasure</term> <note>original: "pleasaunce"; the state of being pleased or satisfied.</note> of the Father; for the bliss, the <term>glory</term> <note>original: "worship"; the honor and reverence due to Christ’s divinity.</note> of the Son; and for the endless delight, the Holy Spirit. The Father is pleased, the Son is honored, and the Holy Spirit <term>delights</term> <note>original: "liketh"; finds pleasure or satisfaction.</note>. And here I saw, in a *third* observation, him in his blessed <term>passion</term> <note>original: "passion"; the suffering of Christ leading up to and during the crucifixion.</note>; that is to say, the joy and the bliss that make him delight in it.

These four were shown, as previously mentioned, for the pains of the passion. And the *fifth* was shown for the joy and the bliss of the passion; for it is God's will that we have true delight with him in our salvation. 

And in this, he desires that we be powerfully comforted and strengthened. And thus he desires <term>joyfully</term> <note>original: "merely"; in this context, it means merrily or with gladness, rather than "only."</note>, through his grace, that our soul be occupied; for we are his bliss, and in us he delights without end. And so shall we delight in him, by his grace. All that he does for us, and has done, and ever shall do, was never a cost or a burden to him, nor could it ever be; but only that he died in our <term>humanity</term> <note>original: "man-"; the text breaks here, likely continuing as "manhood" or "human nature."</note>—

# The Tenth Revelation.

## The Twenty-fourth Chapter.

**W**ITH a joyful expression our good Lord looked into his side, and gazed with joy; and with his sweet looking, he led the understanding of his creature <note>Julian refers to herself in the third person as "his creature" to emphasize her humility before God.</note> through the same wound into his side. And there he showed a fair and delightful place, large enough for all mankind that shall be saved to rest in peace and in love. And with that, he brought to mind his precious, dear blood, and the holy water which he allowed to pour out for love. And with that sweet gazing, he showed his blessed heart split in two; and through his rejoicing, he showed to my understanding a part of the blissful <term>Godhead</term> <note>The essence or nature of God; the Divine Being as opposed to his human form.</note>, as far as he...

[The Lord] would at that time strengthen the poor soul <note>Julian often refers to the human soul, or her own soul, as "poor" to highlight its smallness in comparison to God’s greatness.</note> so that it could understand, as it may be said; that is to mean, the endless love that was without beginning, and is, and shall be forever. as if he had said,

> ‘*My darling, behold and see your Lord, your God, who is your maker and your endless joy. How could it be now that you would ask anything of me that pleases me, but that I should most gladly grant it to you? For my delight is your holiness, and the endless joy and bliss you have with me.*’

This is the simple understanding, as best as I can say, of this blessed word: ‘*Look, how I loved you.*’ Our good Lord showed this to make us glad and merry.
