# The Eleventh Revelation.

## *And the Twenty-fifth Chapter.*

AND with this expression <note>original: "chear"; meaning face or outward appearance</note> of mirth and joy, our good Lord looked down to his right side and brought to my mind the place where our Lady <note>The Virgin Mary</note> stood at the time of his Passion <note>The suffering and crucifixion of Jesus</note>, and said, “Will you see her?” And in this sweet word, it was as if he had said, ‘*I know well that you want to see my blessed mother; for after myself, she is the highest joy that I could show you, the most pleasing and honorable <note>original: "worship"</note> to me, and she is desired most of all to be seen by all my blessed creatures <note>A "creature" in this context refers to any created being, including humans and angels.</note>.*’ 

And because of the marvelous, high, and singular <note>Unique or special</note> love that he has for this sweet maiden, his blessed mother our lady Saint Mary, he shows her bliss and joy; through the meaning of this sweet word, it was as if he said, ‘*Will you see how I love her, so that you might rejoice with me in the love that I have for her, and she for me?*’ 

And also for a deeper understanding, our good Lord speaks this sweet word in love to all of humanity who shall be saved, as if speaking to just one person; as if he said, ‘*Will you see through her how you are loved? For the sake of your love I have made her so high, so noble, and so worthy; and this pleases me, and so I wish that it pleases you too.*’

The *first* was as she conceived; the *second* was as she was in her sorrow under the cross; and the *third* was as she is now—in pleasure, honor <note>original: "worship"</note>, and joy.

# The Twelfth Revelation.

## *The Twenty-sixth Chapter.*

AND after this, our Lord showed himself more glorified to my sight than I saw him before; in which I was taught <note>original: "learned"; in this period, the word was used for both teaching and learning.</note> that our soul shall never have rest until it comes into him, knowing that he is full of joy, intimate <note>original: "homely"; Julian uses this term frequently to describe God’s familiar, comfortable, and unpretentious accessibility to the soul.</note> and courteous, and blissful, and true life. Oftentimes our Lord Jesus said,

> ' *I am he, I am he, I am he who is highest. I am he whom you love. I am he whom you enjoy <note>original: "likest"; to take pleasure or delight in someone.</note>. I am he whom you serve. I am he for whom you long. I am he whom you desire. I am he who is your purpose <note>original: "meanest"; the object of your intent or the one you aim for.</note>. I am he who is all. I am he whom Holy Church preaches to you and teaches you. I am he who showed myself before to you.* '

The number of the words exceeds my wits and understanding, and all my powers; for they were in the highest degree to my sight. For within them is contained I cannot tell what; but the joy that I saw in the showing of them exceeds all that a heart can think, or a soul may desire. And therefore these words are not fully explained here, but let every person, according to the grace that God gives them in understanding and longing, receive them in our Lord’s meaning.

<summary>In the Twelfth Revelation, Julian experiences a vision of Christ in a state of increased glory and hears a rhythmic declaration of his identity as the source of all love and desire. She emphasizes that the joy of this revelation is beyond human description and must be felt through individual grace.</summary>

## The Thirteenth Revelation.

### *The Twenty-seventh Chapter.*

AND after this, our Lord brought to my mind the longing that I had for him before; and I saw nothing hindering <note>original: "lettet"; to prevent or stand in the way.</note> me but sin. And so I considered it generally in us all; and it seemed to me that if sin had not existed, we should all have been clean and like our Lord as he made us. And thus in my folly, before this time, I often wondered why, through the great aforementioned wisdom of God, the beginning of sin was not prevented <note>original: "lettet".</note>; for then it seemed to me that all should have been well. This impulse <note>original: "stirring"; a mental or spiritual movement or thought.</note> was very much to be rejected; and nevertheless, I mourned and sorrowed for it without reason or discretion. But Jesus, who in this vision informed me of all that I needed, answered with this word and said: ‘*Sin is necessary <note>original: "behovely"; fitting, suitable, or needful in a certain context. This is one of the most famous and debated passages in Julian’s writing.</note>, but all shall be well, and all shall be well, and every kind of thing shall be well.*’

In this plain <note>original: "naked"; simple or unadorned.</note> word "sin," our Lord brought to my mind generally all that is not good; and the shameful contempt <note>original: "despite"; scorn or mockery.</note>, and the uttermost tribulation that he bore for us in this life, and his dying; and all his pains and passion, spiritual <note>original: "ghostly"; relating to the spirit or soul.</note> and bodily; and the pains of all his creatures, spiritual and bodily. For we are all in part troubled, and we shall be troubled, following our master Jesus, until we are fully purified <note>original: "purged".</note>.

<summary>Julian begins the Thirteenth Revelation by questioning why God allowed sin to exist. In response, Jesus provides his famous assurance that although sin is "behovely" (necessary or fitting), ultimately "all shall be well."</summary>

<keywords>Thirteenth Revelation, Julian of Norwich, sin, behovely, all shall be well, problem of evil, spiritual suffering, Christ's passion</keywords>

<page-num>62</page-num>

->THE THIRTEENTH REVELATION.<-

of our mortal <note>original: "deadly"; subject to death or belonging to our fallen state.</note> flesh, and of all our inward inclinations <note>original: "affections"; the internal movements of the soul or feelings.</note>, which are not very good. And I saw the contemplation of this along with all the pains that ever were, or ever shall be. And with all this, I understood the passion of Christ to be the greatest and surpassing <note>original: "overpassing"; exceeding all others.</note> pain. All this was shown in a brief touch and quickly passed over into comfort; for our good Lord did not want the soul to be afraid of this hideous sight.

But I did not see sin; for I believe it has no kind of substance, nor any part of being, nor can it be known except by the pain that it causes. <note>This is a central point in Julian’s theology: sin is "nothing" in terms of existence; it is a lack of good rather than a created thing itself.</note> And this pain is something real to my sight for a time, for it purifies us and makes us know ourselves, and ask for mercy. For the passion of our Lord is comfort to us against all this, and so is his blessed will. Because of the tender love that our good Lord has for all who shall be saved, he comforts them quickly and sweetly, saying this: ‘*It is true that sin is the cause of all this pain; but all shall be well, and all manner of things shall be well.*’ 

These words were shown very tenderly, showing no kind of blame to me, nor to any who shall be saved <note>original: "safe".</note>. Then it would be a great lack of gratitude <note>original: "unkindness"; in the Middle English sense of being "unnatural" or ungrateful for the kinship God offers.</note> for me to blame or question <note>original: "wonder on"; to look at with skeptical doubt.</note> God for my sin, since he does not blame me for sin. And in these same words, I saw a high and marvelous secret <note>original: "privity"; a private or hidden matter.</note> hidden in God; this secret he shall openly reveal and make known to us in heaven. In that knowing, we shall truly see the cause why he allowed sin to enter. In that sight, we shall have endless joy.

<summary>Julian reflects on the nature of sin, concluding that it has no "substance" of its own and is only known through the pain it causes, which eventually serves to purify the soul. She repeats the famous promise that "all shall be well" and hints at a divine secret that will explain the existence of sin when revealed in heaven.</summary>

## *The Twenty-eighth Chapter.*

In this way I saw how Christ has compassion for us because of sin. And just as I was previously filled with pain and compassion during Christ's Passion, so in this moment I was partially filled with compassion for all my <term>fellow Christians</term> <note>original: "even Christen"; a Middle English term meaning our equals in faith or fellow human beings.</note>; for he loves those who shall be saved very dearly. That is to say, God’s servants and the Holy Church shall be shaken in sorrow, anguish, and tribulation in this world, just as a cloth is shaken in the wind. And regarding this, our Lord answered, showing me in this way: ‘*Ah, I shall make a great thing of this in heaven—of endless honor <note>original: "worship"; in this context, it refers to the dignity or glory bestowed by God rather than the act of prayer.</note> and everlasting joy.*’

Indeed, I saw so clearly that our Lord rejoices in the tribulation of his servants with pity and compassion. To every person whom he loves and intends to bring to his bliss, he lays upon them something that is not a flaw in his sight, but through which they are humbled <note>original: "lowed".</note> and despised in this world, scorned, mocked, and cast out. And he does this to prevent <note>original: "let"; in older English, this meant to hinder or stop.</note> the harm they would otherwise suffer from the pomp, pride, and vainglory of this wretched life, and to prepare their way to come to heaven in everlasting bliss without end. For he says: ‘*I shall completely break you away from your vain attachments <note>original: "affections"; the internal movements of the soul or emotional fixations.</note> and your wicked pride, and after that, I shall gather you, and make...*’

<summary>Julian reflects on how God allows the "Holy Church" and his servants to endure earthly suffering and humiliation as a means of protection against pride. This tribulation is seen by God with compassion and is ultimately transformed into great honor and joy in heaven.</summary>

*“...you meek and mild, clean and holy by being <term>united</term> <note>original: "oning"; a Middle English term for the process of becoming one with God.</note> to me.”* And then I saw that every natural compassion that a person has for their <term>fellow Christian</term> <note>original: "even Christian"; our equals in faith or fellow human beings.</note> out of love is Christ within them. Every way that Christ was <term>stripped of dignity</term> <note>original: "naughting"; the act of being made into nothing or profoundly humbled, as Christ was during his execution.</note>—which was shown in his passion—was shown again here in this compassion. In this, there were *two kinds of understanding* in our Lord’s meaning: *one* was the bliss to which we are brought, in which he wants us to rejoice. The *other* is for comfort in our pain; for he wants us to know that everything shall turn into <term>honor</term> <note>original: "worship"; here meaning the glory or dignity bestowed by God.</note> and profit for us by the power of his passion. He wants us to know that we suffer nothing at all alone, but with him, and to see him as our foundation. And he wants us to see that his pains and his tribulation so far surpass everything we might suffer that it cannot be fully conceived. A deep contemplation of this will save us from resentment and despair when we feel our own pains. And even if we see truly that our sin deserves the pain, his love still excuses us; out of his great <term>generosity</term> <note>original: "courtesie"; a term from the culture of knighthood that Julian uses to describe God’s refined, gentle, and noble grace.</note> he removes all our blame, and looks upon us with compassion and pity, as if we were innocent and blameless children.

BUT in this I stood looking at things generally, sorrowfully and mournfully, saying to our Lord in my mind with great awe: “Ah, good Lord, how can everything be well...”

He also showed that this is openly known in all the Holy Church on earth. Furthermore, he taught me that I should behold the glorious <term>atonement</term> <note>original: "asseethe"; a Middle English legal and theological term meaning to give "enough" (satisfaction) to make up for a debt or a wrong.</note>; for this <term>making of amends</term> <note>original: "asseeth-making"</note> is more pleasing to the blessed Divinity, and more honorable for man’s salvation—without any comparison—than Adam’s sin was ever harmful. Then our blessed Lord meant this, in this teaching: that we should take heed to this. ‘*For since I have made things well despite the greatest harm; then it is my will that you know thereby that I shall make well all that is less.*’

...counseled and taught inwardly by the <term>Holy Ghost</term> <note>The Holy Spirit.</note>, and outwardly by Holy Church in the same grace. And thus may we see and enjoy that our portion is our Lord. *The other part* is hidden and <term>locked away</term> <note>original: "sparred"; barred or shut fast.</note> from us; that is to say, all that exists besides our salvation. For that is our Lord’s <term>private counsel</term> <note>original: "privy counsel"; a term referring to a monarch's secret advisors or confidential plans. Julian uses it to describe God’s hidden mysteries.</note>, and it belongs to the royal lordship of God to keep his private counsels in peace. 

And it belongs to his servants, out of obedience and reverence, not to wish to know his secrets. Our Lord has pity and compassion on us because some <term>creatures</term> <note>In Julian’s time, this referred to any created beings, especially humans.</note> make themselves so busy with such things; and I am sure that if we knew how greatly we would please him and ease ourselves by leaving it alone, we would. 

The saints in heaven wish to know nothing except what our Lord wishes to show them; also, their charity and their desire are ruled by the will of our Lord. And in this way, our will ought to be like theirs; then we shall want and desire nothing but the will of our Lord, just as they do; for we are all one in God’s purpose. And here I was taught that I should only rejoice in our blessed Savior Jesus, and trust in him for all things.

<summary>Julian explains that while some truths are revealed for our salvation, other "private counsels" of God remain hidden, and we should find peace in not knowing them. She encourages believers to align their wills with God's, just as the saints do, and to find total joy in Christ.</summary>

## *The Thirty-first Chapter.*

And so our good Lord answered all the questions and doubts that I might have, saying very comfortingly: ‘*I am able to make all things well; and I know how to make all things well; and I shall make all things well; and I will make all things well; and you shall see for yourself that every kind of thing shall be well.*’ 

Where he says, ‘*I am able,*’ <note>original: "I may." In Middle English, "may" specifically denoted power and ability.</note> I understand this to refer to the Father; and where he says, ‘*I know how,*’ <note>original: "I can." In Julian's time, "can" meant to have the knowledge or wisdom to do something.</note> I understand the Son; and where he says, ‘*I will,*’ I understand the <term>Holy Ghost</term> <note>The Holy Spirit.</note>; and where he says ‘*I shall,*’ I understand the unity of the blessed Trinity—three persons and one truth. And where he says, ‘*You shall see for yourself,*’ I understand the coming of all mankind that shall be saved into the blissful Trinity. 

And in these five sayings, God wills that we be enclosed in rest and peace. And in this way shall the <term>spiritual</term> <note>original: "ghostly"; in Julian's time, "ghostly" always meant spiritual or relating to the soul, rather than something frightening.</note> thirst of Christ have an end; for this is the spiritual thirst of Christ: the <term>longing of love</term> <note>original: "love-longing"; a recurring theme in Julian's writing, describing God’s intense, nurturing desire for union with humanity.</note> that lasts and ever shall, until we see that sight on <term>Judgment Day</term> <note>original: "dooms-day"; the final judgment at the end of time.</note>. For we who shall be saved, and shall be Christ’s joy and his bliss, are still here, and some are yet to come, and so shall some be until that day. Therefore this is his thirst, and his longing of love for all of us together here in him, leading to our endless bliss, as I saw it; for we are not now fully as whole in him—

<summary>Julian reflects on Christ's famous promise that "all shall be well," breaking down how each part of the sentence relates to the power, wisdom, and will of the Trinity. She introduces the concept of Christ's "spiritual thirst" as his ongoing desire for the salvation of all souls.</summary>

### The Thirty-second Chapter.

At one time our good Lord said, '*All manner of things shall be well:*' and another time he said, '*You yourself shall see that all manner of things shall be well.*' And in these two sayings the soul took <term>different</term> <note>original: "sundry."</note> ways of understanding. One was this: that he wants us to <term>know</term> <note>original: "wit."</note> that he not only takes heed of noble and great things, but also of little and small things, of low and simple things, and of both the one and the other. And he means this when he says, '*All manner of things shall be well;*' for he wants us to know that even the smallest thing shall...

<summary>Julian explains Christ's "spiritual thirst" as a divine longing for humanity that mirrors our own longing for Him, and introduces the famous assurance that "all manner of things shall be well." This assurance applies not just to great matters, but to the smallest and simplest details of existence.</summary>

...not be forgotten. Another understanding is this; that there be many evil deeds done in our sight, and such great harms taken, that it seems to us that it were impossible that it should ever come to a good end. And upon this we look, sorrowing and mourning; so that we cannot rest ourselves in the blissful <term>beholding</term> <note>original: "beholding"; refers to a focused spiritual gaze or contemplation of God.</note> of God as we should do. And the cause is this; that the use of our reason is now so blind, so low, and so simple, that we cannot know the high marvelous wisdom, the power, and the goodness of the blissful Trinity. And this is what he means where he says, ‘*You shall see yourself, that all manner of things shall be well.*’ As if he said: take this now faithfully and trustfully, and at the last end you shall truly be in the <term>fullness</term> <note>original: "fulhead"; an archaic term meaning completeness or the state of being full.</note> of joy. 

And thus in the same five words said before, ‘*I may make all things well;*’ I understand a mighty comfort regarding all works of our Lord God that are yet to come. There is a deed which the blissful Trinity shall perform on the last day, as I saw it; and what that deed shall be, and how it shall be done, is unknown to all creatures who are beneath Christ, and shall be until the time when it is done. The goodness and the love of our Lord God wills that we <term>know</term> <note>original: "wit"; to have knowledge or awareness.</note> that it shall be; and his power and wisdom, by that same love, wills to <term>conceal</term> <note>original: "hill"; an old English word meaning to cover, wrap, or hide from view.</note> it, and hide from us what it shall be and how it shall be done. This is the great deed ordained by our Lord God from before the beginning, treasured and hidden in his blessed breast, known only to himself; by this deed, he shall make all things well. AND yet in this I desired, as much as I dared, that I might have some sight of hell and of purgatory. It is God’s will that we pay great regard to all the deeds he has already done; for he wants us to know, trust, and believe through them everything that he shall yet do. But we must always stop staring at what that future deed shall be; let us instead desire to be like our brothers and sisters, the saints in heaven, who want nothing but God’s will. Then we shall only find joy in God, and be <term>well apaid</term> <note>Satisfied, content, or pleased.</note> both with what is hidden and what is revealed. For I saw truly in our Lord’s meaning that the more we busy ourselves to know his <term>privities</term> <note>Secrets or hidden, private matters belonging only to God.</note> in this or any other thing, the further we shall be from knowing them.

## *The Thirty-fourth Chapter.*

Our Lord showed two kinds of <term>secrets</term> <note>Middle English "privities." These are divine mysteries or hidden truths of God.</note>. One is this great secret, with all the hidden points belonging to it: and it is His will that we know these secrets are hidden in this way until the time that He will clearly show them to us. *The other* are the secrets which He Himself showed openly in this revelation; for those are secrets which He wants to make open and known to us: for He wants us to <term>understand</term> <note>Middle English "wit." To know or perceive clearly.</note> that it is His will that we know them. They are secrets to us, but not only because He wants them to be secrets to us; rather, they are secrets to us because of our spiritual blindness and our ignorance. And therefore He has great <term>pity</term> <note>Middle English "ruth." Compassion for the human condition.</note>; and therefore He Himself will make them open to us, so that we may know Him, and love Him, and cling to Him. For everything that is <term>profitable</term> <note>Middle English "speedeful." Beneficial for our spiritual growth and salvation.</note> for us to understand and to know, our good Lord will most courteously show us what it is, along with all the preaching and teaching of <term>Holy Church</term> <note>The established Christian community and its authoritative doctrines.</note>. God showed the very great <term>pleasure</term> <note>Middle English "pleasaunce." The delight or satisfaction God takes in His followers.</note> that He has in all men and women who strongly and wisely accept the preaching and teaching of Holy Church; for He Himself *is* Holy Church; He is the foundation, He is the substance, He is the teaching, He is the teacher, He is the goal, and He is the <term>reward</term> <note>Middle English "meed." The spiritual prize or payment for a life of faith.</note> for which every natural soul labors. And this is known, and shall <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>Julian explains that God holds two types of secrets: those that remain hidden until the end of time, and those He reveals to us now to overcome our ignorance. She emphasizes that God is the very essence and foundation of the Church's teaching.</summary>

## *The Thirty-eighth Chapter.*

AND God showed that sin shall not be a cause of shame, but of **honor** <note>Middle English: "worship." In Julian's time, this word often meant honor, dignity, or status, rather than just religious adoration.</note> for mankind; for just as every sin is answered by a specific pain in truth, so too is every sin answered by a specific bliss given to that same soul through love. Just as various sins are punished with various pains according to how grave they were, so shall they be rewarded with various joys in heaven for their victories, based on how much pain and sorrow the sin caused the soul on earth. For the soul that shall go to heaven is so precious to God, and that place is so full of honor, that God’s goodness never allows a soul destined for heaven to sin to the point of final loss.

But which sinners are to be so rewarded is made known in the Holy Church on earth, and also in heaven, by surpassing honors; for in this vision my understanding was lifted up into heaven. And then God cheerfully brought to my mind David, and countless others from the **Old Testament** <note>Middle English: "Old Law."</note>. And from the **New Testament** <note>Middle English: "New Law."</note>, he brought to my mind first Mary Magdalene, Peter, and Paul, Thomas, and Jude, **Saint John of Beverley** <note>An 8th-century English saint and Bishop of York. His inclusion here is notable as a "local" example for Julian’s audience.</note>, and many others also without number; how they are known in the church on earth along with their sins; and yet it brings them no shame, but all of it has been turned into honor for them. And therefore our **gracious Lord** <note>Middle English: "courteous Lord." Julian uses the language of the royal court to describe God’s noble and gentle treatment of the soul.</note>

<summary>Julian is shown that for those who are saved, their past sins are transformed by God into a source of heavenly joy and honor rather than eternal shame. For it is necessary that every sinful soul be healed by these medicines; though once a soul is healed, its wounds are seen before God not as wounds, but as <term>honors</term> <note>Original: "worships." In Julian’s time, this referred to the glory or dignity bestowed upon someone, rather than the act of religious worship itself.</note>. And so, conversely, as we are punished here with sorrow and with penance, we shall be rewarded in heaven by the <term>courteous</term> <note>Original: "courteous love." Julian frequently uses the language of the royal court to describe God’s character, suggesting He is a noble, refined, and gracious Lord who treats His subjects with immense respect.</note> love of our God Almighty. He wills that no one who arrives there shall lose any part of their <term>labors</term> <note>Original: "travel." This implies both physical work and the painful struggle or "travail" of the spiritual life.</note> in any degree: for He regards sin as sorrow and pain to His lovers, whom He does not blame because of His love. The <term>reward</term> <note>Original: "meed." A traditional term for payment or compensation earned through effort.</note> that we receive shall not be small, but it shall be high, glorious, and honorable; and so all shame shall turn into honor and into joy.

For our courteous Lord does not want His servants to despair because of frequent failing or grievous falling; for our falling does not stop Him from loving us. Peace and love are always in us, existing and working; but we are not always in peace and in love ourselves. But He wants us to take heed of this: that He is the foundation of our whole life in love. And furthermore, that He is our everlasting Keeper, and mightily defends us against all our enemies that are very <term>cruel</term> <note>Original: "fell." Meaning deadly, fierce, or sinister.</note> and very fierce towards us. And so our need is all the greater, because we give our enemies the opportunity by our falling.

<summary>Julian explains that for those who are saved, God transforms the "wounds" of sin into heavenly honors. She emphasizes that God does not blame His lovers for their failings, but instead acts as a constant, "courteous" protector who rewards the soul's earthly struggles.</summary>

### *The Fortieth Chapter.*

AND this is the supreme friendship of our gracious Lord: that He keeps us so tenderly while we are in our sin. Furthermore, He touches us very secretly and shows us our sin by the sweet light of mercy and grace. But when we see ourselves as so foul, then we <term>imagine</term> <note>Original: "wene." And then we hope that God has forgiven us our sin—and it is true. Then our gracious Lord shows Himself to the soul joyfully and with a completely glad expression, with a friendly welcome as if the soul had been in pain and in prison, saying this:

->‘ *My dear darling, I am glad you have come to me in all your woe. I have always been with you, and now you see me loving you, and we are <term>united</term> <note>Original: "oned." A key term for Julian, referring to "oneing"—the mystical and essential union between the soul and God.</note> in bliss.* ’<-

<summary>Julian describes how God secretly sustains the soul during sin and eventually leads it through contrition to a joyful, loving reunion. She emphasizes that God is never truly angry, but welcomes the repentant soul like a "dear darling" into mystical union.</summary>

And because we beseech him <term>earnestly</term> <note>Original: "mightily."</note> to do that thing which pleases him, it is as if he said: ‘*How could you please me more than to beseech me earnestly, wisely, and <term>willingly</term> <note>Original: "wilfully." In this context, it means with one's full will and purpose.</note> to do the thing that I will have done?*’ And thus the soul by prayer is <term>harmonized</term> <note>Original: "accorded."</note> with God. 

But when our <term>courteous</term> <note>Julian frequently describes God as "courteous," reflecting the medieval ideal of noble, gentle, and respectful conduct.</note> Lord by his special grace shows himself to our soul, we have that which we desire; and then, for that time, we do not see what more we should pray for, but all our intent with all our <term>faculties</term> <note>Original: "mights." In Julian's time, these were the powers of the soul: memory, understanding, and will.</note> is set entirely upon the <term>contemplation</term> <note>Original: "beholding." Here, the word means the act of looking upon or seeing God.</note> of him. And this is a high, <term>imperceptible</term> <note>Original: "unperceiveable."</note> prayer, as I see it; for the whole reason why we pray is to be <term>united</term> <note>Original: "oned." Julian’s signature term for "onement" or the mystical union of the soul with God.</note> into the sight and beholding of him to whom we pray—marveling and rejoicing with <term>reverent awe</term> <note>Original: "reverent dreed." This is not a fear of punishment, but the overwhelming awe felt in the presence of the Divine.</note>, and such great sweetness and delight in him, that we can pray for nothing at all except as he moves us at that time. And well I <term>know</term> <note>Original: "wot."</note> that the more the soul sees of God, the more she desires him by grace; but when we do not see him so, then we feel the need and the cause to pray because of our feeling <note>of his absence</note> and because of our own <term>inability</term> <note>Original: "unableness."</note> to reach Jesus.

<summary>Julian explains that prayer is a gift from God that harmonizes the soul's will with His own, leading to a state of "oning" or mystical union. She describes a high form of prayer where the soul ceases to ask for things and simply rests in the contemplative beholding of the Divine.</summary>

For when a soul is tempted, troubled, and left to herself by her unrest, then it is time to pray to make herself supple and <term>buxom</term> <note>Original: "buxom." And thus I saw that whenever we see a need for which we pray, then our Lord God follows us, helping our desire; and when we, by his special grace, plainly behold him, seeing no other needs, then we follow him. And he draws us to him by love, for I saw and felt that his marvelous and <term>abundant</term> <note>Original: "fulsome." And then we can do no more but behold him, and rejoice with a high, mighty desire to be entirely <term>united</term> <note>Original: "oned." <summary>Julian explains that prayer does not change God's mind, but rather makes the human soul flexible and obedient to His unchanging love. She describes the goal of life as a growing spiritual union ("oning") with God that begins with earthly glimpses and ends in total, eternal immersion in the Divine.</summary>

## *The Forty-fourth Chapter.*

GOD showed in all the Revelations many times that a holy person works his will and his <term>honor</term> <note>Original: "worship." And how this is, I hope by the grace of the Holy Spirit to say as I saw. Truth sees God, and wisdom beholds God; and from these two comes the third, and that is a marvelous delight in God, which is love. Where truth and wisdom are truly, there is love truly coming from them both, and all of it is God’s making: for God is endless sovereign truth, endless sovereign wisdom, and endless sovereign love unmade. And a human soul is—

### *The Forty-sixth Chapter.*

And yet, throughout all this time from the beginning to the end, I had *two* ways of <term>beholding</term> <note>Original: "be-holdings." Julian is describing two different perspectives or spiritual viewpoints she held simultaneously.</note>: the *one* was endless, continuous love, with the certainty of being kept safe and a blissful salvation—for this was the focus of the entire <term>revelation</term> <note>Original: "shewing."</note>. Instead, I was taught within it to love and appreciate it, so that with the help of our Lord and His grace, I might increase and rise to more heavenly knowledge and higher love.

And so, in all this contemplation, it seemed to me that it was necessary to see and know that we are sinners, and do many evils that we ought to avoid, and leave many good deeds undone that we ought to do. Therefore, we deserve pain, blame, and wrath.

And notwithstanding all this, I saw truly that our Lord was never angry, nor ever shall be. For He is God: He is good, He is truth, He is love, and He is peace. His power, His wisdom, His charity, and His unity do not allow Him to be angry. For I saw truly that it is against the <term>essential nature</term> <note>Original: "property." In a theological sense, a property is a quality that is inseparable from a thing's essence.</note> of His power to be angry, and against the nature of His wisdom, and against the nature of His goodness. God is that good—

<summary>Julian reflects on the paradox of God's mercy; while she was taught that mercy is the forgiveness of God's anger, her visions reveal a God who is never angry. ### *The Forty-eighth Chapter.*

BUT our good Lord the <term>Holy Spirit</term> <note>Original: "Holy Ghost." In Julian’s time, "Ghost" referred to the spirit or breath of God.</note>, who is endless life dwelling in our soul, truly protects us, and works a peace within, and brings the soul to ease by grace, and makes it <term>pliant</term> <note>Original: "buxom." And either it comes from a [false] feeling of power, or a feeling of wisdom, or a feeling of goodness—which feeling is not of God, but is on our part; for we, through sin and wretchedness, have a wrath within us and a constant opposition to peace and love. 

And that he showed very often in his lovely <term>expression</term> <note>Original: "chear." This refers to the look on God's face or his outward demeanor toward the soul.</note> of <term>compassion</term> <note>Original: "ruth."</note> and pity. And this was shown in such a way that I could not perceive the property of mercy in any other way than as if it were all love within love. ### *The Forty-ninth Chapter.*

**For** it was a great wonder to the soul, which was continually shown in everything, and with great diligence—beholding that our Lord God, as far as He Himself is concerned <note>Original: "aneynst himself."</note>, cannot forgive, because He cannot be angry. The servant came: in the servant is contained the Second Person of the Trinity; and in the servant is also contained Adam—that is to say, all of humanity. And therefore when I say "the Son," it means the Divine Nature, which is <term>equal</term> <note>Original: "even." In this context, it refers to the theological concept of the Son being co-equal with the Father.</note> with the Father. God’s Son fell with Adam into the "valley" of the Virgin’s womb (who was the fairest daughter of Adam), and that was in order to excuse Adam from blame in heaven and on earth. 

And with great power, He rescued him from hell. By the wisdom and the goodness that were in the servant, we understand God’s Son; by the poor clothing, like a laborer standing near the left side, we understand the humanity of Adam, with all the <term>misfortune</term> <note>Original: "mischief." The servant: and thus our good Lord Jesus has taken all our blame upon himself. And therefore our Father neither can nor will assign more blame to us than he does to his own beloved and worthy Son, Jesus Christ.

Father. For this reason, this meaning was shown in the understanding of the <term>humanity of Christ</term> <note>Original: "manhood of Christ." ## THE FOURTEENTH REVELATION.

...and [standing] on the left side signifies that he was not yet fully worthy to stand directly before the Lord. His "starting" was the <term>Divine nature</term> <note>Original: "God-head."</note>, and his "running" was the <term>Human nature</term> <note>Original: "Man-head." Julian uses the image of "running" to describe Christ’s swift journey from heaven to the womb and then to the cross.</note>; for the Divine nature leaped from the Father into the virgin’s womb, falling into the taking on of our human nature. And in this falling, he took on a great soreness: the "sore" that he took was our flesh, in which, as soon as he received it, he felt deadly pains. 

The fact that he stood fearfully before the Lord, and not directly in front of Him, signifies that his clothing was not respectable enough to stand directly before the Lord; nor could it be, nor should it have been his duty while he was a laborer. Nor could he sit with the Lord in rest and peace until he had rightfully won his peace through his hard labor. And by "the left side," it is meant that the Father deliberately left His own Son in his humanity to suffer all of humanity's pain without sparing him. 

The fact that his <term>tunic</term> <note>Original: "kirtle." A basic garment, here representing the human body of Christ.</note> was on the verge of being ragged and torn refers to the rods and scourges, the thorns and the nails, the drawing and the dragging, and the tearing of his tender flesh. And by his wallowing and writhing, groaning and mourning, it is understood that he could never rise in all his might from the time he had fallen into the virgin’s womb until his body was slain and dead, and he yielded his soul into the Father's hand, along with all of humanity for...

### THE FOURTEENTH REVELATION.

...to whom he was sent. And at this point, he first began to show his might; for then he went into hell. And when he was there, he raised up the <term>great root</term> <note>This refers to the "Harrowing of Hell," where Christ is said to have rescued the souls of the righteous—starting with Adam, the "root" of humanity—from the depths.</note> out of the deep deepness, which was rightfully joined to him in high heaven. And our foul, mortal flesh, which <term>God’s Son</term> took upon himself—which was <term>Adam’s old tunic</term> <note>Original: "Adams old kirtle." Julian uses the "kirtle" or basic garment as a metaphor for human nature—here described as "strait" (tight or narrow) and "short" to signify our fallen, limited state.</note>, narrow, bare, and short—was then made by our <term>Savior</term> fair, new, white, and bright, and of endless purity. It was made wide and long, fairer and richer than the clothing which I saw on the <term>Father</term>, for that clothing was blue <note>In Julian's vision, the Father's blue clothing represents the vastness of the heavens and steadfastness.</note>. 

And <term>Christ’s</term> clothing is now of a fair, seemly <term>medley of colors</term> <note>Original: "medelour." This suggests a variegated or shimmering beauty, representing the many redeemed souls who now "clothe" Christ.</note>, which is so marvelous that I cannot <term>describe</term> <note>Original: "descrie."</note> it, for it is made entirely of true honor. Now the Lord does not sit on earth in a wilderness, but he sits on his rich and noble seat which he made in heaven, most to his liking. 

Now the <term>Son</term> does not stand before the <term>Father</term> like a servant before a lord, fearfully clothed and <term>partially naked</term> <note>Original: "party naked." ### THE FOURTEENTH REVELATION.

Our reason is grounded in God, who is substantially kindness <note>original: "kindness." In Julian’s time, this word meant both "nature" and "goodwill." She is saying God is the very essence of our nature.</note>. From this substantial kindness, mercy and grace spring and spread into us, working all things to fulfill our joy. These are our foundations <note>original: "grounds."</note> in which we have our being, our growth, and our fulfillment; for in nature <term>kind</term> <note>Julian uses "kind" to refer to our "kinship" with God through creation—our natural, God-given state.</note>, we have our life and our being; and in mercy and grace, we have our growth and our fulfillment. These are *three properties in one goodness*: and where one works, all work in those things that belong to us.

God wants us to understand this, desiring with all our heart and with all our strength to have knowledge of them ever more and more, until the time that we are fulfilled. For to know them fully and to see them clearly is nothing else but the endless joy and bliss that we shall have in heaven, which God wants us to begin here by knowing his love. For we cannot progress by our reason alone unless we also have memory and love alongside it; nor can we be saved only by our natural foundation <term>kindly ground</term> <note>The inherent, essential connection to God that exists within every soul from the moment of creation, which Julian believes can never be lost.</note> that we have in God, unless we also have mercy and grace coming from that same foundation. 

For from these three workings, altogether we receive all our good things; of which, the first are the goods of nature <note>original: "kind." These are the inherent gifts of the soul given at creation, such as reason and the capacity to love.</note>. For in our first making, God gave us as much good, and as great a good, as we might receive in our spirit alone; but his foreseeing purpose in his endless wisdom intended that we should be double <note>Julian refers here to our "double" nature: our eternal soul (substance) and our physical/sensory life (sensuality). Both are part of God's design.</note>.

<summary>Julian explains that the human soul is built upon three divine foundations: nature, mercy, and grace. She emphasizes that while we were created with a perfect "spirit," God’s wisdom intended for us to have a "double" nature that experiences both the eternal and the earthly.</summary>

### *The Fifty-seventh Chapter.*

AND regarding our <term>substance</term> <note>The "substance" is the higher, eternal part of the soul that Julian believes is never separated from God.</note>, he made us so noble and so rich that we always perform his will and bring him honor; when I say "we," I mean the human beings who shall be saved. For truly I saw that we are those whom he loves, and we do what pleases him, eternally and without stopping. And from this, great riches and high, noble <term>virtues</term> <note>original: "vertues." In Julian’s time, these were not just good habits, but spiritual powers or strengths flowing from God into the soul.</note> come in measured portions to our soul at the moment it is joined to our body; in this joining, we are made <term>sensual</term> <note>Our "sensuality" refers to our physical existence and the lower part of the soul that experiences the world through the senses.</note>.

And so, in our substance we are complete, but in our sensuality we fall short; this failing God will restore and fulfill by the working of mercy and grace, which flow abundantly into us from his own natural goodness. Thus, this natural goodness causes mercy and grace to work within us. And the natural goodness that we have from him <term>enables</term> <note>original: "ableth." To make one capable or fit to receive God's gifts.</note> us to receive the working of mercy and grace.

I saw that our nature is entirely in God; from this nature, he creates diverse things that flow out of him to perform his will. His nature preserves us, and his mercy and grace restore and fulfill us. And none of these shall perish; for our nature, which is the higher part, was joined to God at our creation, and God was joined to our nature, which is the lower part, when he took on our human flesh. And thus in Christ our two natures are <term>united</term> <note>original: "oned." ...beloved children, who are <term>buxom</term> <note>original: "buxom." In Julian’s time, this meant being compliant, yielding, or obedient to God's will.</note> and obedient to him. Julian’s specific term for being made one, or united, with God.</note> and all made into a perfect human being. And by the yielding and giving in the grace of the Holy Spirit, we are fulfilled. Our <term>substance</term> <note>In Julian’s theology, the "substance" is the higher, eternal part of the human soul that remains forever united with God in heaven.</note> is in our Father, God Almighty; and our substance is in our Mother, God all-wisdom; and our substance is in our Lord God the Holy Spirit, all-goodness; for our substance is whole in each person of the Trinity, which is one God.

And our <term>sensuality</term> <note>The "sensuality" is the lower part of the soul—our physical, embodied human experience on earth.</note> is only in the Second Person, Christ Jesus, in whom is the Father and the Holy Spirit. And in him, and by him, we are mightily taken out of hell and out of the wretchedness on earth, and worshipfully brought up into heaven, and blessedly united to our substance—increased in riches and nobility by all the virtue of Christ and by the grace and working of the Holy Spirit.

<summary>Julian explains how the Holy Spirit rewards and fulfills the soul, and how human nature—both its eternal substance and its earthly sensuality—is united and perfected within the Trinity through Jesus Christ.</summary>

### *The Sixty-third Chapter.*

HERE we may see that we have, <term>by nature</term> <note>original: "verily of kind."</note>, a reason to hate sin. And we have, <term>by grace</term> <note>original: "verily of grace."</note>, a reason to hate sin; for <term>nature</term> <note>original: "kind."</note> is entirely good and beautiful in itself. Grace was sent out to save nature, to keep nature, to destroy sin, and to bring beautiful nature back to the blessed point from which it came—that is, God—with even more nobility and honor through the virtuous working of grace. For it shall be seen before God, by all His holy ones in joy without end, that nature has been <term>tested</term> <note>original: "assaid." Literally "assayed," like a metal being tested for purity in a furnace.</note> in the fire of tribulation, and in that fire no lack or defect was found. 

Thus, nature and grace are in agreement; for grace is God, just as <term>uncreated nature</term> <note>original: "unmade kind." Julian refers to the divine essence that was never created because it is God Himself.</note> is God. He is two in His manner of working, but one in love. Neither of them works without the other, nor can they be separated. And when we, by the mercy of God and with His help, bring ourselves into accord with nature and grace, we shall see truly that sin is worse, viler, and more painful than hell, beyond any comparison. For it is contrary to our beautiful nature; for as truly as sin is unclean, so truly is sin <term>unnatural</term> <note>original: "unkind." In Middle English, this word meant both "cruel" and "against nature." For Julian, to sin is to act against our own biological and spiritual essence.</note>. And this is a horrible thing to see for the loving soul that desires to be entirely beautiful and shining...

<summary>Julian explores the harmony between human nature and divine grace, arguing that because our nature is essentially good, sin is a painful and "unnatural" violation of our true selves. She explains that while nature and grace work differently, they are both expressions of God's single love.</summary>
<keywords>nature, grace, sin, Mother Christ, tribulation, unmade kind, salvation, Julian of Norwich, Middle English theology</keywords>

<summary>Julian describes Jesus as a nurturing Mother who heals the soul with His blood and "bears" humanity into eternal life through the Cross. She emphasizes that the relationship between the soul and God should mirror the natural trust and humility a child has for its mother.</summary>

## The Fifteenth Revelation.

### The Sixty-Fourth Chapter.

**AND** then shall the bliss of our <term>Motherhood</term> <note>original: "Mother-head." ## *The Sixty-fifth Chapter.*

AND thus I understood that whatever man or woman willfully chooses God in this life for love, they may be sure that they are loved without end, with an endless love that works that grace within them. For he wills that we hold to this trustfully: that we be as <term>certain</term> <note>original: "sicker." Sure, secure, or certain.</note> in our hope for the bliss of heaven while we are here, as we shall be in <term>certainty</term> <note>original: "surety."</note> when we are there. And the more pleasure and joy we take in this <term>assurance</term> <note>original: "sickerness."</note>, with reverence and humility, the better it pleases him.

<summary>Julian concludes the Fifteenth Revelation by explaining that earthly suffering is brief and should be viewed lightly in comparison to God's eternal reward. She emphasizes that choosing God in this life provides a certain hope of heaven that should bring the believer great joy.</summary>

...sitteth in rest: the <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "God-head." The divine essence or nature of God.</note> ruleth and giveth without any instrument or <term>effort</term> <note>original: "business." Here meaning labor or taxing work.</note>. And the soul is entirely occupied with the blessed Godhead; that is, supreme might, supreme wisdom, and supreme goodness. The place that Jesus takes in our soul he shall never leave, for all eternity, as I saw it: for in us is his <term>most intimate home</term> <note>original: "homliest home." In Julian's Middle English, "homely" meant familiar, intimate, and deeply belonging, rather than plain or unattractive.</note>, and his endless dwelling. And in this he shows the pleasure he takes in the creation of the human soul; for just as the Father was able to make a creature, and just as the Son was able to make a creature, so too did the Holy Spirit desire that the human soul be made, and so it was done.

And therefore the blessed Trinity rejoices forever in the creation of the human soul: for he saw from before time began what would please him without end. Everything he has made demonstrates his Lordship, just as understanding was granted at that time through the example of a <term>created being</term> <note>original: "creature."</note> who is led to see great nobility and kingdoms belonging to a Lord. And when they had seen all the nobility below, then marvelling, they were moved to seek upward to that high place where the Lord dwells; knowing by reason that his dwelling is in the most excellent place.

And thus I truly understand that our soul can never have rest in things that are beneath itself. And when it rises above all created things into itself, yet it cannot remain in the contemplation of itself; but all its contemplation is blessedly fixed on God, the Maker who dwells within it; for in the human soul is his true dwelling place. And the highest light and the brightest...

<summary>Julian explains that the human soul is God's favorite dwelling place, created by the shared will of the Trinity. She concludes that because God dwells within us, the soul can never find true rest in anything less than God himself.</summary>

The radiance of the city is the glorious love of our Lord God, as I saw it. And what can make us rejoice more in God than to see that he rejoices in us, the highest of all his works? And just as in the first word that our good Lord showed—referring to his blessed passion—"by this, the devil is defeated," so he said in the last word, with full and true faithfulness referring to us all: "You shall not be overcome." And so he desires that we love him and take delight in him, and powerfully trust in him, and all shall be well. And soon everything was closed, and I saw no more after this.

### *The Sixty-ninth Chapter.*

THE <term>devil</term> <note>original: "fiende."</note> came again with his heat and with his stench, and kept me very busy. The stench was so vile and so painful, and the physical heat was also terrifying and exhausting.

## *The Seventieth Chapter.*

I fixed my physical eyes on the same cross where I had seen comfort before that time. My tongue spoke of Christ’s passion and repeated the faith of the Holy Church, and I fastened my heart on God with all the trust and the strength I could muster, saying to myself: "You are working very hard now to keep yourself in the faith, so that you will not be captured by your enemies. If you were to be this busy from now on and forevermore to keep yourself from sin, that would be a <term>sovereign occupation</term> <note>original: "soveraign occupation." A supreme, excellent, or most important way to spend one's time and energy.</note>." For I faithfully believed that if I were safe from sin, I would be completely safe.

### *The Seventy-second Chapter.*

**ALL** this blessed teaching of our Lord God was revealed by *three* parts; that is to say, by <term>physical vision</term> <note>original: "bodily sight." Julian uses this to describe visions seen with her literal eyes.</note>, and by words formed in my understanding, and by <term>spiritual vision</term> <note>original: "ghostly sight." This refers to a direct, non-visual understanding or impression made upon the soul.</note>. Regarding the physical vision, I have described what I saw as truly as I can; and regarding the words, I have relayed them exactly as our Lord revealed them to me; and regarding the spiritual vision, I have said something of it, but I can never fully describe it. And therefore concerning this spiritual vision I am moved to say more, as God will give me grace.

God revealed *two* kinds of sickness that we have: the *one* is <term>impatience</term> <note>original: "unpatience."</note> or <term>sloth</term> <note>original: "slouth." In a medieval context, sloth (or acedia) was a spiritual sorrow or apathy that made divine things feel burdensome.</note>; for we bear our <term>toil</term> <note>original: "travel." Meaning labor, struggle, or the weariness of life.</note> and our pain heavily. The *other* is despair or <term>fearful doubt</term> <note>original: "doubtful dread."</note>, as I shall explain later. Generally, he revealed sin, in which all is included, but in particular he revealed nothing but these two. And these two are what most <term>exhaust</term> <note>original: "travelleth."</note> and trouble us, according to what our Lord revealed to me. He desires us to be healed of these; I speak of those men and women who for the love of God hate sin, and dispose themselves to do God’s will. Then, because of our <term>spiritual blindness</term> <note>original: "ghostly blind-head."</note> and physical heaviness, we are most inclined toward these. And therefore it is God’s will that they be recognized, and then we should reject them, just as we do other sins. And as a help against this, full meekly our Lord revealed the patience that he had in his hard passion: and

## *The Seventy-third Chapter.*

FOR I understood *four* types of fear: *one* is the fear of a <term>sudden fright</term> <note>original: "fray." A sudden shock or disturbance to the senses.</note> that comes to a person suddenly through human frailty. This fear does good, for it helps to cleanse a person, just as bodily sickness does, or any other pain that is not sin; for all such pains help a person if they are accepted patiently. The *second* is the fear of pain, by which a person is stirred and awakened from the sleep of sin; for a person who is deep in the sleep of sin is not able, at that time, to receive the soft comfort of the Holy Spirit until they have experienced this fear of pain, of bodily death, and of <term>spiritual enemies</term> <note>original: "ghostly enemies."</note>. And this fear stirs us to seek comfort and mercy from God. And thus, this fear helps us as an entrance, and makes us able to have <term>contrition</term> <note>The state of feeling remorseful and penitent for sins.</note> through the blissful touching of the Holy Spirit. The *third* is <term>doubtful fear</term> <note>original: "doubtful dread." An anxious uncertainty about God's love or one's own salvation.</note>: doubtful fear is significant because it draws one toward despair. God wants it to be turned in us into love, through a true knowledge of love; that is to say, that the bitterness of doubts be turned into the sweetness of <term>natural love</term> <note>original: "kind love." ### *The Seventy-seventh Chapter.*

OUR Lord, in his mercy, shows us our sin and our weakness through the sweet, gracious sight of himself. For our sin is so foul and so horrible, that he, in his courtesy, will not show it to us except by the light of his mercy. He wills that we have knowledge of four things: the *first* is that he is the foundation from whom we have all our life and our being. The *second* is that he protects us powerfully and mercifully during the time we are in our sin, among all our enemies who are very <term>fierce</term> <note>original: "fell." Meaning cruel, deadly, or savage.</note> toward us. And we are in so much more peril because we give them the opportunity to attack, and we do not know our own need. The *third* is how courteously he protects us, and makes us to know...

The light is the cause of our life; the night is the cause of our pain and all our sorrow. In this sorrow, we desire an endless <term>reward</term><note>original: "meed."</note> and thanks from God, because through mercy and grace we willfully acknowledge and believe in our light, walking within it wisely and with strength. And at the end of sorrow, our eye shall suddenly be opened, and in the clarity of sight, our light shall be full. This light is God our Maker, Father, and Holy Spirit, in Christ Jesus our Savior. Thus I saw and understood that our faith is our light in our night, and this light is God, our endless day.

### *The Eighty-third Chapter.*

THIS light is charity <note>In Julian’s time, "charity" (from the Latin *caritas*) referred to the highest form of spiritual love, rather than just giving to the poor.</note>, and the measuring of this light is done for our benefit by the wisdom of God. For the light is not so great that we can see our blessed day clearly, nor is it entirely <term>shut out</term><note>original: "speered." Meaning locked, fastened, or excluded.</note> from us; but it is such a light...

<summary>Julian describes faith as a spiritual light that guides the soul through the "night" of earthly suffering. She emphasizes that our reason is a gift from God, grounded in our very nature, and that Christ acts as a motherly guide leading us toward the full light of eternity.</summary>
<keywords>faith as light, Motherhood of Christ, reason, nature, charity, spiritual darkness, Julian of Norwich, Sixteenth Revelation</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Sixteenth Revelation’s discussion on the "Light" of faith from Chapter 83 and begins Chapter 84. Julian transitions from the concept of faith as a guiding light to the three-fold nature of Charity (Divine Love) and God’s eternal, unchanging love for humanity.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>
<header>THE SIXTEENTH REVELATION.</header>

...in which we may live <term>rewardingly</term><note>original: "meedfully." This refers to living in a way that earns "meed," or a spiritual reward from God.</note> through our <term>labor</term><note>original: "travel." In Julian’s time, this meant hard work, toil, or the arduous journey of life.</note>, looking forward to the honorable thanks of God. And this was seen in the <term>sixth showing</term><note>original: "vith shewing." Julian refers back to a specific earlier vision where she saw God welcoming the soul into heaven with gratitude.</note>, where he says, ‘*I thank you for your service, and for your labor.*’ Thus love keeps us in faith and in hope; and faith and hope lead us into love; and at the end, all shall be love. I had *three* ways of understanding this light of love: the *first* is uncreated love; the *second* is created love; the *third* is love given. Uncreated love is God; created love is our soul in God; love given is virtue, and that is a graceful gift of action; through which we love God for his own sake, and ourselves in God, and everything that God loves for God’s sake.

### *The Eighty-fourth Chapter.*

AND in this vision I marveled greatly; for despite our simple living and our blindness here, our <term>courteous</term><note>original: "courteous." Julian uses this term to describe God’s noble, gentle, and respectful character toward his creatures.</note> Lord looks upon us in this work endlessly, rejoicing. And of all things, we may please him best by wisely and truly believing this, and by rejoicing with him and in him. For as truly as we shall be in the bliss of God without end, praising and thanking him, just as truly have we been in the foresight of God, loved and known in his endless purpose from before the beginning. In this uncreated love he made us; in the same love he keeps us, and never allows us to be hurt in a way that would diminish our bliss. And there—

<summary>Julian defines three forms of love—God himself, the soul in God, and the gift of virtue—while explaining that God has loved humanity since before the beginning of time.</summary>
<keywords>Charity, Divine Love, Sixth Showing, Virtue, Eternity, Predestination, Divine Joy, Foresight</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page concludes Chapter 84 and begins the final chapter of Julian’s text, Chapter 85. The text moves from the soul’s joyful belief in God’s goodness to the ultimate revelation of God’s hidden mysteries at the end of time, concluding with Julian’s famous reflection on the meaning of her visions—which is Love.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>
<header>THE SIXTEENTH REVELATION.</header>

...[there]fore when the <term>judgment</term><note>original: "doome." In Middle English, this refers to the Last Judgment at the end of the world.</note> is given, and we are all brought up above, then shall we clearly see in God the <term>secrets</term><note>original: "privities." These are the hidden mysteries or private counsels of God that are currently concealed from human understanding.</note> which now are hidden from us. And then shall none of us be moved to say of anything, ‘Lord, if it had been this way, it would have been well.’ But we shall all say with one voice, ‘Lord, blessed may you be, for it is thus: thus it is well. And now we see truly, that all things are done as they were your <term>decree</term><note>original: "ordinance." This refers to God's eternal plan or sovereign arrangement of all things before the beginning of time.</note> before anything was made.’

### *The Eighty-fifth Chapter.*

**T**his book is begun by God’s gift and his grace; but it is not yet <term>finished</term><note>original: "performed." Julian suggests that while the writing exists, the full spiritual realization or "performance" of the visions is a continuing process in the life of the believer.</note> as I see it. For charity’s sake, let us all pray together, with God's working—thanking, trusting, and <term>rejoicing</term><note>original: "enjoying." In Julian’s usage, this means finding joy in God or participating in divine happiness.</note>. For our good Lord wants to be prayed to, according to the understanding that I took from all his own meaning. And in the sweet words where he says full merrily, ‘*I am the ground of your beseeching.*’<note>This is one of Julian’s most famous quotes, indicating that God is the origin and the foundation of the very desire to pray.</note> For truly, I saw and understood in our Lord’s meaning, that he showed it because he wants it to be known more than it is. In which knowledge he will give us grace to love him and cleave to him. For he beholds his heavenly treasure and solace in heavenly joy, drawing our hearts from the sorrow and darkness that we are in. And from the time that it was shown, I desired often to know what our Lord’s meaning was; and fifteen years after and more, I was answered in <term>spiritual</term><note>original: "ghostly." In the 14th century, "ghostly" was the standard word for "spiritual" or "pertaining to the soul."</note> understanding...

<summary>Julian concludes that at the Last Judgment, all of God's hidden plans will be revealed as perfect, and she begins her final reflection on how her book is a gift of grace intended to lead readers to love and prayer.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, divine providence, secrets of God, prayer, ground of beseeching, spiritual understanding, Julian of Norwich</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Sixteenth Revelation and the entire manuscript. It captures the famous "Love was his meaning" passage, where Julian reflects on fifteen years of contemplating her visions before receiving this final, internal clarity on God’s ultimate motive.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>THE SIXTEENTH REVELATION.</header>

understanding, saying thus: “What? Would you <term>know</term><note>original: "wit." In Middle English, this means to know, understand, or be aware of.</note> your Lord’s meaning in this thing? Know it well: love was his meaning. Who shows it to you? Love. Why does he show it to you? For love. Hold yourself therein, and you shall know more of the same. But you shall never know anything else therein, without end.” Thus was I taught that love is our Lord’s meaning. And I saw most surely in this, and in all that our God made for us, that he loved us; which love was never <term>diminished</term><note>original: "slacked." This suggests a love that never loosens, weakens, or grows cold.</note>, nor ever shall be. And in this love he has done all his works: and in this love he has made all things profitable to us: and in this love our life is everlasting; in our creation we had a beginning; but the love in which he made us was in him from before any beginning. In which love we have our beginning. And all this we shall see in God without end.

->Thanks be to God.<note>original: "Deo Gratias."</note><-

->Here ends the book of the Revelations of Julian, Anchorite of Norwich,<note>original: "Explicit liber Revelationum Juliane Anachorite Norwic." An "Anchorite" was a type of religious hermit who lived in a cell attached to a church.</note><-
->on whose soul may God have mercy.<note>original: "cujus anime propicietur Deus."</note><-

### THE END.

<summary>This concluding passage reveals Julian's central theological discovery: that the motive and meaning behind all of God's revelations and actions is eternal, unchanging love. It finishes with a formal scribal ending in Latin, identifying the author and offering a prayer for her soul.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, eternity, creation, anchorite, Norwich, final revelation, God's meaning</keywords>

### * A.

**A. B. C.,** *the basic elements of learning.*
> “Of which greatness he will have us knowing here as if in an A. B. C.” <note>In this context, the "A. B. C." refers to the very beginnings or fundamentals of spiritual understanding, much like a child learning the alphabet.</note>

**Abide,** *to wait for, or to stay.*
**Abiden,** *waited for.*
**Abidings,** *waitings for.*
**Able,** *to enable or help.*
**Abode,** *waited.*
**Accord,** *to harmonize, join, or to agree.*
**Accord,** *harmony or agreement.*
**Adred,** *afraid.*
**Adventure,** *a chance occurrence or contingency.*
**Advice, or Advise,** *consideration, attention, or careful observation.*
**Advisement, or Avisement,** *the same meaning as Advice.*
**After as, or After that,** *accordingly as.*
**Again,** *against.*
**Again-being,** *redemption.* <note>Literally "being again," referring to the restoration of humanity through Christ.</note>
**All,** *altogether.*
**All to,** *entirely.* **All to breaketh:** *entirely breaks.*
**Anaved?** ‘It seemed that he was anaved; that is to say, beginning anew to travel.’ <note>The glossary author seems uncertain of this specific term, interpreting it from context as a fresh start.</note>

**Aneynst, Anent, Anenst, Aneinst, or Anempts,** *opposite, facing, with, according to, or concerning.*
**And,** *if.*
**Anon,** *presently or immediately.*
**Appay,** *to satisfy or recompense.*
**Apaid,** *content or satisfied.*
**Arraid,** *arrayed or dressed.*
**As,** *just as.* <note>original: "omnino sic." A Latin phrase meaning "entirely so" or "altogether thus."</note>
**Assaid,** *tried or tested.*
**Assay, or Assey,** *a trial or test.*
**Asseethe,** *satisfaction.* <note>A theological term for making amends or paying a debt, often used regarding Christ's atonement.</note>
**Asseethe-making,** *making satisfaction.*
**Assoon,** *immediately.*

### B.

**Be,** *been.*
**Be-christened.** ‘Whether they have be-christened or not.’ <note>Meaning "to be baptized."</note>
**Beclipping,** *embracing.* <note>From the Middle English "clippen," meaning to hug or hold closely.</note>
**Beclose,** *to enclose or shut in.*
**Becometh,** *comes.*
**Begrowing,** *growing or increasing.*
**Behaviour,** *outward appearance.* “And the shape at his outward behaviour.” <note>The text notes that Shakespeare uses this word in a similar sense of external manner or conduct.</note>

## G.

**Game**, *ridicule, sport, victory.*

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary for Julian of Norwich’s "Revelations of Divine Love," completing the section for the letter I and covering K, L, and the beginning of M. It focuses on Julian’s distinctive use of Middle English terms, particularly those related to "nature" and "hindrance."</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>
->GLOSSARY.<-

<term>Into</term>, unto.
<term>Into</term>, in two. <note>In this context, "into" refers to being split or divided.</note>
> “That the love of him and of our souls shall never be parted <note>original: "departed"</note> **in two** without end.”
<term>Irkeness</term>, irksomeness. <note>A feeling of weariness or boredom.</note>

### K.

<term>Kild</term>, killed.
<term>Kind</term>, nature, humanity. <note>This is a vital term for Julian, referring to the essential nature of a person or the natural world as created by God.</note>
<term>Kind</term>, or <term>Kindlie</term>, natural, loving.
<term>Kindly</term>, naturally.
<term>Kindle</term>, incite, stimulate, sharpen. 
> “He **stimulates** our understanding.”
<term>Kindness</term>, naturalness, nature?
<term>Kirtle</term>, a tunic or waistcoat.
<term>Knowing</term>, known.

### L.

<term>Lack</term>, impediment, fault, disgrace, want.
<term>Langure</term>, to languish. <note>To grow weak or suffer from longing.</note>
<term>Lean-head</term>, leanness. <note>The suffix "-head" is used here to create a noun meaning "the state of being lean."</note>
<term>Leaver</term>, or <term>levir</term>, rather, more willingly.
<term>Leese</term>, lose.
<term>Leesing</term>, losing.
<term>Lengeth</term>, lengtheneth.
<term>Lessed</term>, lessened.
<term>Let</term>, or <term>Letting</term>, hindrance.
<term>Let</term>, to prevent, hinder. <note>In older English, "to let" meant the opposite of its modern meaning; it meant to stop or block someone.</note>
<term>Lightlier</term>, lighter.
<term>Lightning</term>, enlightening, lighting.
<term>Like</term>, to please, to take pleasure in.
<term>Liken</term>, to like, to please.
<term>Likeness</term>, pleasure, delight.
<term>Likeness</term>, comparison.
<term>Liking</term>, appearance, likeness?
<term>Liking</term>, pleasure.
<term>Liking</term>, pleasurable.
<term>Litle</term> (v.), lead. <note>An archaic past tense form of the verb "to lead."</note>
> “One time my understanding was **led** down into the sea-ground.”
<term>Litle</term>, little.
<term>Litle-head</term>, littleness.
<term>Lively-head</term>, liveliness.
<term>Loath</term>, unwilling.
<term>Loathness</term>, unwillingness.
<term>Long</term>, to belong.
<term>Long</term>, to desire.
<term>Lore</term>, knowledge, doctrine, advice.
<term>Lother</term>, more unwilling.
<term>Lovesome</term>, lovely.
<term>Lowed</term>, humbled.

### M.

<term>Manhead</term>, manhood, humanity. <note>Specifically referring to the human nature of Christ.</note>
<term>Manner</term>, kind or sort. 
> “All **sorts of** virtues” : all kind of virtues.
<term>Marvail</term>, or <term>Marveile</term>, wonder.
<term>Marvel</term>, to wonder.
<term>May</term>, to be able: (used in the infinitive mood) <note>In Julian’s time, "may" often meant "to have the power or ability to."</note>
> “But our precious mother Jesu **is able to** feed us with himself” : Is able to feed us with himself.
<term>Maym</term>, injury. <note>From which we get the modern word "maim."</note>
<term>Mean</term>, intervening, intermediate.
<term>Meane</term>, medium, instrument. 
> “Without any **intermediary**” : immediately.
<term>Meddled</term>, or <term>Medled</term>, mingled, twofold.
<term>Medelur</term>, or <term>Medle</term>, mixture.

<vocab>Into, Kild, Kind, Kindle, Kirtle, Langure, Leaver, Leese, Lengeth, Lessed, Let, Liken, Likeness, Liking, Litle, Lively-head, Lore, Lowed, Manhead, Manner, Marvail, Maym, Meddled, Medelur</vocab>

<summary>This page of the glossary defines Middle English terms used by Julian of Norwich, focusing on words for nature (kind), hindrance (let), and the humanity of Christ (manhead). These definitions clarify Julian's theological language, such as her description of Jesus as a "mother" who is able to feed the soul.</summary>

# GLOSSARY.

<term>Meed</term>, *reward.*
<term>Meedfully</term>, *meritoriously.*
<term>Meek</term>, *to make meek or humble.*
<term>Meek-head</term>, *meekness.* <note>The suffix "-head" is a Middle English way of forming a noun, similar to the modern "-ness."</note>
<term>Mickle</term>, or <term>Mykill</term>, *much.*
<term>Middes</term>, *midst, middle.*
<term>Mid-face</term>, *middle of the face, profile.*
<term>Mid-head</term>, *centre.*
<term>Mid-point</term>, *central point.*
<term>Mids</term>, *midst.*
<term>Might</term>, *power, strength.*
<term>Mightest</term>, *most mighty.*
<term>Mightifully</term>, *with fulness of might.*
<term>Mightilier</term>, *more mighty.*
<term>Mightly</term>, *mightily.*
<term>Mind</term>, *feeling, consideration, remembrance.*
<term>Mischief</term>, *misfortune.*
<term>Mis-liking</term>, *disliking.*
<term>Misrule</term>, *to disorder.*
<term>Mistely</term>, *darkly.*
<term>Moneth</term>, *moaneth.*
<term>Morrow</term>, *morning.*
<term>Most</term>, *perhaps for "moisture."*
> "And also his **moisture** <note>original: "most"</note> clotted <note>original: "cloggering"</note> together and dried to my sight." Chapter xvi. <note>This describes the physical state of Christ's blood during the Crucifixion.</note>

<term>Mote</term>, *must, may.*
<term>Mother-head</term>, or <term>Mother-hood</term>, *state or condition of a mother, all which appertains to a mother.* <note>Julian uses this term to explore the "motherhood" of God and Christ.</note>
<term>Mourningly</term>, *mournfully.*

->N.<-

<term>Naught</term>, *to empty, mock, bring to nothing.*
<term>Naughting</term>, *emptying, excepting?*
<term>Naughting</term>, *nothingness, worthlessness, short-comings?* "Our breakings and our naughtings."
<term>Near-hood</term>, *nearness.*
<term>Ne</term> (conjunction), *nor.*
<term>Ne</term> (adverb), *not.*
<term>Nobility</term>, *dignity, splendor.*
<term>Noble</term>, *perhaps for "nobley."*
> "Which I saw him highly enjoy for the worshipful resting, and **splendor** <note>original: "noble"</note> that he will, and shall bring his servant to by his plenteous grace."

<term>Noblety</term>, or <term>Nobletie</term>, *nobleness: same as Nobility.*
<term>Nobley</term>, or <term>Nobly</term>, *dignity, splendor.*
<term>Nobliest</term>, or <term>Noblest</term>, *most noble.*
<term>None</term>, *probably for* **Anon**. *Purpose, design?*
> "And **immediately** <note>original: "none"</note> after this our blessed Lord gave me again of the comfort and the rest in soul."

<term>Noieth</term>, *annoyeth.*
<term>Not</term>, *naught.*
<term>Not but</term>, *only.*
<term>Nought</term>, or <term>Naught</term>, *nothing.*

->O.<-

<term>Of</term>, *in.*
<term>On</term>, *in.*
<term>One</term>, *to unite.*
<term>One-head</term>, *oneness.*
<term>Oned</term>, <term>Onid</term>, or <term>Onyd</term>, *united.* <note>One of Julian's most important concepts: the "oneing" or union of the soul with God.</note>
<term>Ones</term>, *once.* "All at ones": *all at once.*
<term>Oning</term>, *union.*
<term>Oning</term>, *uniting.*
<term>Or</term>, *before.* "Or that": *before that.*

<vocab>Meed, Mickle, Mid-face, Misrule, Moneth, Naughting, Near-hood, Noblety, Nobley, Oned, Oning</vocab>

<summary>This page defines Middle English terms used in Julian of Norwich’s writings, focusing on words for power, motherhood, and nothingness. It highlights Julian's specific use of 'Oneing' to describe spiritual union and 'Mother-head' to describe the nature of God.</summary>
