<term>Otherwhile</term>, *at other times.*
<term>Ought</term>, *anything.*
<term>Out-passing</term>, *expiring.*
<term>Over</term>, *far above.*
<term>Over-laid</term>, *overborne.*
<term>Over-more</term>, *moreover.*
<term>Over-parte</term>, *upper part.*
<term>Over-pass</term>, *to excel, surpass.*
<term>Over-passing</term>, *excellence.*
<term>Over-passing</term>, *exceedingly.*
<term>Over-yede</term>, *overspread, covered.* <note>An archaic past tense of "over-go."</note>
<term>Owe</term>, *ought.*
<term>Own</term>, *to unite.*
<term>Owned</term>, *united.*

### P.

<term>Paid</term>, *satisfied, contented.*
<term>Painfuller</term>, *more painful.*
<term>Parliament</term>, *consultation, debate.*
<term>Party</term>, *part.*
<term>Pass</term>, *to die.*
<term>Pass</term>, *to surpass, excel.*
<term>Passible</term>, *capable of suffering.*
<term>Passing</term>, *transient.*
<term>Passingly</term>, *exceedingly.*
<term>Paynius</term>, *pagan.*
<term>Peace</term>, *to set at peace.*
<term>Pearcing</term>, *piercing.*
<term>Pellets</term>, *balls.*
<term>Penance</term>, *repentance.*
<term>Perished</term>, *lost, destroyed.* "None shall be perished."
<term>Plainlie</term>, *simply, clearly.*
<term>Pleasaunce</term>, *pleasantness, pleasure, delight.*
<term>Pleasable</term>, *pleasurable, agreeable?*
<term>Plenteous-head</term>, *plenteousness.*
<term>Point, in a</term>, *with exactness, with precision, to a nicety?* "I saw God in a point." "That we set the point of our thought": *that we concentrate our thought?* <note>Julian uses the "point" as a powerful metaphor for God's presence in all things, suggesting that God is the center and the essence of every single moment and object.</note>
<term>Praid</term>, *entreated.*
<term>Prayer</term>, *pray?* "For all the cause wherefore we prayer."
<term>Prime</term>, *morning. It meant originally the first canonical hour of prayer. "Prime day": morning.* <note>The canonical hours were the set times of day for prayer observed by monks, nuns, and anchorites like Julian.</note>
<term>Privities</term>, *secrets, private affairs.*
<term>Pronity</term>, *grovelling.*
<term>Protraid</term>, *portrayed.*

### Q.

<term>Quick</term>, *to make alive.* <note>Similar to the phrase "the quick and the dead," meaning the living and the dead.</note>

### R.

<term>Raced</term>, or <term>Raised</term>, *rased, scraped.*
<term>Raigning</term>, *reigning.*
<term>Raising</term>, *scraping.*
<term>Ready</term>, *prompt, on the alert.*
<term>Redily</term>, *readily.*
<term>Reform</term>, *to make anew.*
<term>Religious person, a</term>, *one of a religious order, a clergyman.* <note>In the Middle Ages, a "religious" person specifically meant someone who had taken holy vows, like a monk, nun, or friar.</note>
<term>Resentment</term>, *regard.*
<term>Reserving</term>? "Reserving the worshipful thanks of God."
<term>Retchlessness</term>, *that is, Wretchlessness, or Recklessness, carelessness.* <note>This term often refers to a spiritual apathy or neglect of one's soul.</note>
<term>Retchlesser</term>, *more reckless, or careless.*
<term>Retchesly</term>, *recklessly, carelessly.*
<term>Reproved</term>, *reprobated?*
<term>Reward</term>, *to award, regard, respect?*

<term>Rewly-head</term>, that is, <term>Ruely-head</term>, *ruefulness, grievousness, sorrowfulness.* <note>The suffix "-head" was an old way of forming nouns, similar to "-ness" or "-hood."</note>
<term>Rialty</term>, *royalty.*
<term>Richless</term>, *reckless, careless.*
<term>Right</term>, *truly, exactly, completely.*
"Right as": *just as.*
<term>Rights</term>, *offices, sacraments.* <note>Likely referring to the "Last Rites" or religious duties.</note>
<term>Right full-head</term>, *plenitude.*
<term>Rightful-head</term>, *rightfulness.*
<term>Rightwise</term>, *truly wise.*
<term>Rimpild</term>, *wrinkled.*
<term>Roode-tree</term>, *cross.* <note>From "Rood," the old English word for a crucifix or cross.</note>
<term>Round-head</term>, *roundness.*
<term>Ruful</term>, or <term>Rufull</term>, *rueful, pitiful, grievous.*
<term>Ruth</term>, or <term>Rewthe</term>, *pity, sorrow.*

## S.

<term>Sad</term>, *firm, grave.* <note>In Julian’s time, "sad" often meant "solid" or "serious" rather than just "unhappy."</note>
<term>Sadly</term>, *firmly, gravely.*
<term>Sadness</term>, *firmness, consistency, gravity.*
<term>Safe</term>, *saved.*
<term>Sagging</term>, *drooping, hanging heavily like a wet cloth.*
<term>Salve</term>. "And he is salve of our life." <note>Likely used here to mean both a healing ointment and salvation.</note>
<term>Same</term>, *end?* "But his wisdom and his love suffer not the same to come until the best time."
<term>Save</term>, *to make good.*
<term>See</term>, *seat.* <note>Referring to a throne or a seat of authority, as in a bishop’s "see."</note>
<term>Seelie</term>, *simple, weak.* <note>The origin of the modern word "silly," which originally meant "blessed" or "innocent."</note>
<term>Self</term>, *same.*
<term>Sennet</term>, <term>Sevenight</term>; *the space of seven nights and days, a week.*
<term>Setled</term>, *settled.*
<term>Shape</term>, *form, figure.*
<term>Shapely</term>, *fit, proper.*
<term>Sharply</term>, *swiftly.*
<term>Shrewdness</term>, *wickedness, ill-nature.*
<term>Shriven</term>, *confessed and absolved.*
<term>Shrowd</term>, *wicked, malicious.*
<term>Sicker</term>, or <term>Siker</term>, *sure.*
<term>Sickerness</term>, or <term>Sikerness</term>, *security, assurance.*
<term>Sickerly</term>, *surely.*
<term>Sickness</term>, *security?* <note>The editor suggests this might be a mistake for "sickerness" (security) in the phrase:</note> "For sickness and comfort against all tribulations that may come."
<term>Side</term>, *long, ample, broad, spacious.*
"Wide and side": *wide and long.*
<term>Sikerest</term>, *most sure.*
<term>Simple-head</term>, *simpleness.*
<term>Single-head</term>, *singleness.*
<term>Sithen</term>, or <term>Sithens</term>, *since, afterwards.*
<term>Slade</term>, *valley, precipice.*
<term>Slouth</term>, *sloth.*
<term>Sodaine</term>, *sudden.*
<term>Sodenlie</term>, or <term>Soudeinlie</term>, *suddenly.*
<term>Soft</term>, *timorous, low, gentle, quiet.*
<term>Somedeal</term>, *somewhat, in some measure.*
<term>Sooth</term>, *truth.*
<term>Soothly</term>, *truly.*
<term>Soothfastness</term>, *truth.*
<term>Soothnesse</term>, *same as Soothfastness.*
<term>Sothfastlie</term>, *assuredly.*
<term>Speed</term>, *profit.*
<term>Speed</term>, *to profit, to dispatch.*
<term>Speedful</term>, *profitable, useful, effectual.*
<term>Standing</term>, *understanding.*

It provides modern readers with the definitions for archaic Middle English terms and grammatical constructions that have changed significantly since the 14th century.</meta>

->GLOSSARY.<-

<term>Standing</term>, *consistent with, in agreement with, or becoming.*
<term>Steed</term>, or <term>Stead</term>, *a resting-place.*
<term>Streit</term>, *straight or close.*
<term>Stint</term>, *to cease or stop.*
<term>Stinted</term>, *ceased or stopped.*
<term>Stinting</term>, *ceasing, stopping, or a limit.*
<term>Stonnyed</term>, *astonished or stunned.*
<term>Strengthed</term>, *strengthened.*
<term>Substanciallie</term>, *with the reality of existence, truly, or really.*
<term>Subtile</term>, *exquisitely spun or exquisitely fine.*
<term>Suffer</term>, *to allow.*
<term>Suffer</term>, *to chasten?* <note>The author questions if the word implies a form of discipline or correction in this context.</note>
<term>Sulloing</term>, *sullying.*
<term>Surety</term>, *sureness or reality.*
<term>Sweinly</term>, *darkly or obscurely.* <note>Related to the Middle English word for a dream or a faint vision.</note>
<term>Swemful</term>, *obscure.*
<term>Swilge</term>, *foul or foulness.*

### T.

<term>Take</term>, *taken.*
<term>Than</term>, *then.*
<term>That</term>, "And all that fair virtues."
<term>That</term> (conjunction), *because.*
<term>That</term>, a demonstrative pronoun used as a relative: *As much as. It is sometimes used in place of "the same."*
<term>The</term>. "And be diseased in divers manners for the own profit." "As it seemeth to the self." <note>In these instances, "the" functions as a reflexive or possessive, meaning "its" or "itself."</note>
<term>Themself</term>, *themselves.*
<term>Then</term>, *than.*
<term>There</term>, *frequently used in the sense of "where."*
<term>Therefro</term>, *therefrom.*
<term>Thereon</term>, *thereon.*
<term>Therto</term>, *thereto.*
<term>Ther</term>, *when used in a compound word, it signifies "that" without necessarily referring to a physical place.*
<term>Tho</term>, *those.*
<term>Threateth</term>, *threateneth.*
<term>To</term>, *when combined with verbs, it generally acts to increase the intensity of the action.* <note>An "augmentative" prefix, similar to how we might use "thoroughly" today.</note>
<term>To fore</term>, or <term>To for</term>, *before.*
<term>Token</term>, *a mark or a plague-spot.* <note>In historical contexts, a "token" could specifically refer to the physical signs of the pestilence.</note>
<term>Travaile</term>, or <term>Travel</term>, *toil, labor, or fatigue.*
<term>Travel</term>, *to labor, to toil, to be in anguish, or to give pain.*
<term>Travellous</term>, *grievous or distressing.*
<term>Treat</term>, *to negotiate, carry on, or settle.*
<term>Trowe</term>, *to think or trust.*

### U.

<term>Unableness</term>, *feebleness or inability.*
<term>Unbegun</term>, *without beginning.*
<term>Unchristen</term>, *unchristian.*
<term>Uncunning</term>, *a want of cunning, or skill.* <note>In Middle English, "cunning" simply meant knowledge or "kenning."</note>
<term>Undeparted</term>, *undivided.* <note>The word "depart" originally meant to separate or part ways.</note>
<term>Under-sett</term>, *propped up.*
<term>Understand</term>, *understood.*
<term>Undertake</term>, *to receive.*
<term>Undiscreetly</term>, *indiscreetly.*
<term>Un-fore-sight</term>, *a want of foresight.*
<term>Unied?</term> *united.*
<term>Uning</term>, *union.*
<term>Unkind</term>, *unnatural.* <note>Literally "against kind," meaning against the natural order.</note>
<term>Unknowing</term>, *ignorance.*
<term>Unlettered</term>, *unlearned.*
<term>Unloathful</term>, *unspotted.*
<term>Unlothful</term>, *willing.*
<term>Unlothfulness</term>, *willingness.*
<term>Unmight</term>, *weakness or impotency.*

<meta>This page continues the glossary for the "Revelations of Divine Love," completing the entries for the letter U and covering V and W. It helps bridge the gap between 14th-century Middle English and modern usage, explaining how common words like "skill" or "utter" had very different meanings in Julian of Norwich's time.</meta>

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>->GLOSSARY. 213<-</header>

<term>Unmighty</term>, *weak.*
<term>Unneath</term>, <term>Unnethes</term>, <term>Unneth</term>, or <term>Uneth</term>, *scarcely.*
<term>Unpassible</term>, *incapable of suffering.* <note>A theological term describing a state, often attributed to God or glorified bodies, where one cannot be affected by pain or external change.</note>
<term>Unpatience</term>, *impatience.*
<term>Unpeace</term>, *disquietude.*
<term>Unpossible</term>, *impossible.*
<term>Unrest</term>, *want of rest, uneasiness, trouble.*
<term>Unsickerness</term>, *insecurity, frailty.* <note>Derived from the word "sicker," which meant sure or certain.</note>
<term>Unskillful</term>, *unreasonable, injurious.* <note>In this period, "skill" often meant reason or discernment; therefore, to be unskillful was to lack reason.</note>
<term>Unwise</term>, *foolish.*
<term>Unwitting</term>, *not knowing.*
<term>Up</term>, *according to.* “And eschew venial (sins) reasonably up our might.” <note>Meaning to avoid minor sins to the best of one's ability or power.</note>
<term>Up-risen</term>, *raised again, in a resurrection state.*
<term>Up-rising</term>, *resurrection.*
<term>Utter</term>, *outward.* <note>Refers to the "outer" person or the physical world as opposed to the "inner" spiritual life.</note>

## V.

<term>Venial</term>, *pardonable.* Venial sin: in contradistinction to mortal sin. <note>In Catholic theology, a "venial" sin is a lesser offense that does not deprive the soul of divine grace, unlike a "mortal" or deadly sin.</note>
<term>Vernacle</term>, diminutive of <term>Veronike</term>, *a copy in miniature of the picture of Christ which is fabled to have been miraculously imprinted on a handkerchief, preserved in the Church of St. Peter, at Rome.* <note>This refers to the Veil of Saint Veronica; "Veronike" is an English form of Veronica.</note>
<term>Verie</term>, or <term>Very</term>, *real, true.*
<term>Verily</term>, *really, truly.*
<term>Vertue</term>, *power, strength; efficacy.* <note>From the Latin "virtus," meaning strength or excellence.</note>
<term>Vertuous</term>, *powerful, efficacious.*

## W.

<term>Wallow</term>, *roll about.*
<term>Ware</term>, *aware.* *“But be we ware.”*
<term>Wax</term>, *grow.*
<term>Weal</term>, *wealth, prosperity, happiness.*
<term>Ween</term>, or <term>Wene</term>, *to suppose, think.*
<term>Went</term>, or <term>Wende</term>, *thought.*
<term>Which</term>, *what.*
<term>Whiles, the</term>, *meanwhile.*
<term>Whistering</term>, *whispering.*
<term>Whole-head</term>, *wholeness, fulness.*
<term>Wide</term>. *“Side and Wide”* : *far and widely.*
<term>Wilfull</term>, *earnest.*

### Y.

<term>Ye</term>, *old form of Yea (Yes).* <note>Used as an affirmative, distinct from the pronoun "ye" meaning "you."</note> “Yes, to such an extent I saw that our Lord rejoices in the suffering of his servants.” <note>original: “Ye so far forth I saw that our Lord enjoyeth of the tribulation of his servants.”</note>
<term>Yield</term>, *to give, or yield a reward; when applied to God, to bless.* “And in our good Lord the Holy Spirit we have our rewarding, and our payment for our living and our labor. <note>original: "Holy Ghost... yielding... travel"</note> So,

<note>This page contains no written text, illustrations, or marginalia. In historical bookbinding, blank pages—known as flyleaves—were commonly placed at the start or end of a manuscript. They acted as a physical buffer to protect the valuable handwritten text from the friction of the heavy wooden or leather covers. They also provided a convenient space where later owners of the book might record their names, lineage, or short prayers, though this particular page has remained untouched for centuries.</note>
